Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-05 Last comment 2026-11-09 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2 0/1] mm, swap: only prefer nonfull clusters on synchronous devices
Show 1 individual patches
  • [1/1] mm, swap: only prefer nonfull clusters on synchronous devices Neutral Submitted 2026-11-05Last comment 2026-11-09 Nhat Pham Kairui Song Alexandre Ghiti Andrew Morton
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a performance regression in the Linux kernel, specifically related to swap I/O operations on synchronous devices like NVMe partitions. The issue arises from two commits: one that prefers non-full clusters over free ones and another that frees swap slots on exclusive write faults. This leads to scattered writes and increased read requests, resulting in decreased throughput. The patch proposes a solution by only preferring non-full clusters on synchronous devices, which improves performance metrics such as device read and write requests per transaction and throughput.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer Nhat Pham suggested considering also preferring non-full clusters for zswap devices, noting it would require a best-effort check of the folio's cgroup's zswappability.

From: Alexandre Ghiti <alex@ghiti.fr>
Subject: mm, swap: only prefer nonfull clusters on synchronous devices
Date: Mon, 09 Nov 2026 15:18:32 -0800

Commit 9a42aed48421 ("mm, swap: prefer nonfull over free clusters") made
the allocator fill nonfull clusters before taking free ones on swap
devices without discard.  Commit 3cedb745d354 ("mm: clarify the
folio_free_swap() for do_swap_page()") frees the swap slot on an exclusive
write fault.  Such faults now leave holes in formerly full clusters,
putting those clusters back on the nonfull list.  Reclaim allocates slots
one folio at a time, scattering a batch across the holes instead of
producing mergeable writes.  Later swapin can also require more read
requests.

On a mysql/sysbench workload in a 256 MiB memcg swapping to an NVMe
partition, 7.3 compared with the previous version produced 59% more device
read requests and 27% more write requests per transaction.  Throughput was
unchanged without iocost and fell 19% with iocost.

Prefer free clusters first on nonsynchronous devices, preserving
contiguous swap slots for merging I/O.  Keep nonfull-first on synchronous
devices such as zram: they do not merge I/O, and filling holes saves free
clusters for large folios.  Keep free-cluster preference on discard
devices to spread writes.

In the same workload, v2 reduced read requests per transaction by 14% and
write requests by 48% against its base kernel.  Throughput was unchanged
without iocost and improved 33% with iocost.  The RFC's batch-aware
allocator also improved this workload, but this policy needs no batch
information from reclaim.  Reverting the slot-freeing change would keep
swap space pinned under dirty pages; a proposed 50% swap-usage cutoff for
free-cluster preference was arbitrary and would stop helping as swap
filled.

The 7.0 to 7.2 kernels retained the swap slot after a write fault as a
side effect of 4b34f1d82c65 ("mm, swap: free the swap cache after folio is
mapped").  The observed regression requires both 9a42aed48421 and
3cedb745d354; stable backports should target trees containing both.

Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261105155032.240457-1-alex@ghiti.fr
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261105155032.240457-2-alex@ghiti.fr
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261022145615.2184343-1-alex@ghiti.fr

 mm/swapfile.c |   19 +++++++++++++------
 1 file changed, 13 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)

--- a/mm/swapfile.c~mm-swap-only-prefer-nonfull-clusters-on-synchronous-devices
+++ a/mm/swapfile.c
@@ -973,6 +973,17 @@ static bool __swap_cluster_alloc_entries
 	return true;
 }
 
+/*
+ * Prefer a new cluster over a nonfull one if the device needs discard, to
+ * spread out the writes, or if it is not synchronous, to keep the slots
+ * of a swapout batch contiguous so that its writes can be merged.
+ */
+static bool swap_prefer_free_cluster(struct swap_info_struct *si)
+{
+	return (si->flags & SWP_PAGE_DISCARD) ||
+	       !(si->flags & SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO);
+}
+
 /* Try use a new cluster for current CPU and allocate from it. */
 static unsigned int alloc_swap_scan_cluster(struct swap_info_struct *si,
 					    struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
@@ -1143,11 +1154,7 @@ static unsigned long cluster_alloc_swap_
 	}
 
 new_cluster:
-	/*
-	 * If the device need discard, prefer new cluster over nonfull
-	 * to spread out the writes.
-	 */
-	if (si->flags & SWP_PAGE_DISCARD) {
+	if (swap_prefer_free_cluster(si)) {
 		found = alloc_swap_scan_list(si, &si->free_clusters, folio, false);
 		if (found)
 			goto done;
@@ -1159,7 +1166,7 @@ new_cluster:
 			goto done;
 	}
 
-	if (!(si->flags & SWP_PAGE_DISCARD)) {
+	if (!swap_prefer_free_cluster(si)) {
 		found = alloc_swap_scan_list(si, &si->free_clusters, folio, false);
 		if (found)
 			goto done;

Boris Burkov

Patches Submitted (2)
Last comment 2026-11-12 [PATCH] btrfs: fix block group ref leak when scrub races with unused bg deletion Neutral LLM

This patch fixes a block group reference leak in Btrfs when scrub operations race with unused block group deletion. The issue arises from concurrent access to the `unused_bgs` list, where a block group can be added back to it after being removed. To fix this, the patch introduces a check for an empty list before taking a new reference on the block group.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Qu Wenruo ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Approved the patch with a Reviewed-by tag. The fix looks correct and should prevent the reference leak issue.
Show original comment 

\u5728 2026/11/12 10:38, Boris Burkov \u5199\u9053:
> btrfs_delete_unused_bgs() takes a block group off fs_info->unused_bgs
> with list_del_init() and, when discard is on, later moves it onto the
> transaction's deleted_bgs list and takes a reference for that list
> membership. Meanwhile, it is possible for scrub to add it back to the
> unused block groups list:
> 
>           CLEANER                               SCRUB
>    btrfs_delete_unused_bgs()
>      list_del_init(&bg->bg_list)
>                                          scrub_enumerate_chunks()
>                                            btrfs_mark_bg_unused(bg)
>                                              if (list_empty(&bg->bg_list))
>                                                btrfs_get_block_group(bg)
>                                                list_add_tail(unused_bgs)
>      list_move(&bg->bg_list, deleted_bgs)
>      btrfs_get_block_group(bg)
> 
> Other users of list_move() for bg->bg_list (like btrfs_mark_bg_unused
> itself) ensure that the list was empty under the bg spin lock to decide
> whether it merits a fresh reference for being linked to the list, and
> we need to apply that pattern here as well.
> 
> Note that this is not abstracted to a helper function because some of
> the callers have additional logic deciding whether to link it or not and
> I think a twice used, twice unused helper would be more confusing than it
> would be helpful.
> 
> Reported-by: Sashiko <sashiko-bot@kernel.org>
> Link: https://sashiko.dev/#/bug/linux-821034ac-556f-4d72-af2c-d04468879af8
> Signed-off-by: Boris Burkov <boris@bur.io>

Reviewed-by: Qu Wenruo <wqu@suse.com>

Signals: APPROVED
[PATCH] btrfs: fix double release of data space on failed direct IO COW write Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a double release of data space on failed direct IO copy-on-write (COW) writes in the Btrfs file system. The issue occurs when a COW write fails after reserving data space, causing the reserved extent to be freed and then releasing all allocated data space again. This results in an underflow of available data space, leading to warnings and clamping to zero. The patch records the consumed length during reservation and releases only the remaining allocated data space on error, preventing the double release and fixing the issue.

Last comment 2026-11-10 Re: [PATCH v6 05/25] btrfs: zoned: use the regular allocator for conventional metadata block groups Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses an issue where replacing a device in a Btrfs filesystem could lead to illegal allocations due to the BLOCK_GROUP_FLAG_SEQUENTIAL_ZONE flag not being updated correctly. The problem arises when a block group is created on a device with conventional zones and then replaced with a device having sequential zones, causing the system to hang or allocate blocks incorrectly. To fix this, the patch likely updates the allocator to handle such replacements more robustly.

Gregory Price

51 items
Patches Submitted (5)
[PATCH] drm/nouveau/dmem: free migration arrays on early fault returns Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a memory leak in the nouveau_dmem_migrate_to_ram function by properly freeing migration arrays when migrate_vma_setup fails or returns no migratable pages. The issue was caused by the introduction of THP support, which changed the allocation method for these arrays without updating the error handling. The patch preserves the existing behavior for setup errors and empty migrations.

Last comment 2026-11-13 [PATCH] mm/hmm: reject shadow-stack mappings Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch rejects shadow-stack mappings in the Heterogeneous Memory Management (HMM) module to prevent devices from gaining write access to user-space shadow stacks on x86 architectures.

How does this look for an altered changelog?

: On x86, a shadow-stack PTE has its ordinary write bit clear.  The CPU
: rejects ordinary stores to it, but pte_write() reports it writable because
: shadow-stack instructions can write it.  HMM consequently sets
: HMM_PFN_WRITE, which Nouveau turns into ordinary GPU write permission.  A
: process with GPU access can then overwrite its own shadow stack,
: potentially corrupting return addresses or bypassing that protection.
: 
: Reject shadow-stack VMAs in hmm_vma_walk_test(), including read access.  A
: requested fault returns -EFAULT; a snapshot records HMM_PFN_ERROR.

Last comment 2026-11-13 [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: ignore a bind policy whose nodes have no memory Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a problem where a bind policy is treated as movable-only if its nodes have no memory, leading to allocation failures and VM fault leaks. The patch ignores such policies until the nodes regain memory, preventing these issues. This fix targets systems with CPUSETS=n and HOTREMOVE=y configurations.

[PATCH 00/22] mm: introduce N_MEMORY_COMMON 22 patches

  • [18/22] mm/compaction: restrict compaction to common memory
  • [19/22] mm/migrate: restrict move_pages() destinations to common memory
  • [20/22] PM: hibernate: size the image from common memory
  • [21/22] mm/khugepaged: base the recommended min_free_kbytes on common memory
  • [22/22] nodemask: remove the now-unused first_memory_node/next_memory_node
Neutral LLM

This patch series introduces N_MEMORY_COMMON, a new concept for general-purpose memory separate from node memory (N_MEMORY). It creates a baseline to ensure private node memory is default opted-out of being touched by the MM, similar to ZONE_DEVICE. The series consists of three phases: introducing N_MEMORY_COMMON, defining memory not part of the general pool (N_MEMORY_PRIVATE), and adding feature bits for userland management.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Gregory Price (Meta) Neutral LLM
No substantive comments or reviews have been made on the patch series so far.
Last comment 2026-11-10 [PATCH v5 0/7] sched/numa: stop VMA scan filters from gating promotion 7 patches

  • [1/7] mm: support promotion-only NUMA hinting scans
  • [2/7] mm: allow shared folios to be promoted to a fast tier
  • [3/7] sched/numa: scan read-only file mappings in tiering mode
  • [4/7] sched/numa: separate VMA placement from scan continuation
  • [5/7] sched/numa: scan PID-inactive VMAs for promotion Neutral Gregory Price (Meta)
  • [6/7] mm: use BIT() for change_protection() flags Neutral Lorenzo Stoakes (ARM)
  • [7/7] mm: use VMA flag helpers in NUMA balancing Neutral Lorenzo Stoakes (ARM) Gregory Price (Meta)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses an issue in NUMA balancing where VMA scan filters were preventing memory-tier promotion, leading to unexpected bandwidth utilization and poor performance on certain workloads. The problem was identified when testing fairness controls and found that numa balancing was broken due to recent shmem fixes. The patches plumb the scan reasoning into the prot_none injection while retaining placement-scan filtering, allowing for correct promotion of hot memory to local nodes. The series includes several related cleanups and is designed to be backported to stable kernels.

On Fri, Nov 13, 2026 at 03:41:33PM -0500, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> On 2026-11-13 16:03 -0400, Jason Gunthorpe wrote:
> > On Fri, Nov 13, 2026 at 02:55:36PM -0500, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> > > HMM can grant ordinary device write access to a userspace shadow stack.
> > > On x86, pte_write() is true for a shadow-stack PTE, so a populated page
> > > can be returned with HMM_PFN_WRITE without taking a CPU write fault.
> > 
> > I'm not sure I understand this? The pte is writable but you don't want
> > only DMA to write to it?
> >
> > Is there something special on x86 that makes the shadow stack pte
> > non-writable by the CPU?
> >
> 
> Some of the context is here:
> https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261111-mason-migrate-et-al-fixes-v1-10-3d2454362e8d@kernel.org/

> We had a similar issue with migration, but this should handle the DMA
> case as well.
> 
> It's "technically writable" but the kernel is supposed to prevent that.

Bah, it's not writable at all. The CPU PTE has write disabled and
someone thought it was a good idea to make pte_write() return true
because the CPU allows writes through a special instruction on this
special PTE? So now all the places like this think its writable, when
it really isn't.

You could also fix this by having something like pte_real_write() or
whatever that doesn't lie about the write bit in the PTE :|

The patch is still fine, in that it matches what everything else does,
but I'd include these details someplace..

Jason

How does this look for an altered changelog?

: On x86, a shadow-stack PTE has its ordinary write bit clear.  The CPU
: rejects ordinary stores to it, but pte_write() reports it writable because
: shadow-stack instructions can write it.  HMM consequently sets
: HMM_PFN_WRITE, which Nouveau turns into ordinary GPU write permission.  A
: process with GPU access can then overwrite its own shadow stack,
: potentially corrupting return addresses or bypassing that protection.
: 
: Reject shadow-stack VMAs in hmm_vma_walk_test(), including read access.  A
: requested fault returns -EFAULT; a snapshot records HMM_PFN_ERROR.

Show original comment 
On 11/13/26 1:04 AM, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> On 2026-11-11 12:55 -0500, Tal Zussman wrote:
>> migrate_vma_setup() installs a migration entry for each page it
>> collects, which carries the uffd bit over to the new PTE. However, a PTE
>> that maps the zero page is left in place without a migration entry.
>> migrate_vma_insert_page() later overwrites it with a new PTE that is
>> built only from the VMA flags, so the uffd-wp bit or the RWP PAGE_NONE
>> protection of the old PTE is lost. The same applies to the huge zero
>> PMD.
>> 
>> Once a driver has migrated such a zero page, writes and accesses to it
>> no longer fault into userfaultfd, so the manager misses them. With async
>> tracking, PAGEMAP_SCAN unconditionally reports the page as written or
>> accessed.
>> 
>> This requires a driver that migrates memory to a device.
>> 
>> To fix it, do not mark a zero page for migration if its entry has the
>> uffd bit set in a VMA registered for WP or RWP. The driver sees an
>> address that was not migrated, which drivers already have to deal with.
>> 
>> The uffd bit can also be set after the zero page was collected, as
>> write-protecting only takes the mmap lock for read. So check again under
>> the page table lock in migrate_vma_insert_page() and
>> migrate_vma_insert_huge_pmd_page().
>> 
> 
> Similar to last patch, this changelog could be improved.  My best
> attempt at summarizing:
> 
> """
> Device migration replaces a zero-page mapping with a fresh entry,
> losing its userfaultfd WP or RWP protection.
> 
> Don't migrate the uffd-protected zero pages.
> 
> Skip protected zero pages during collection. Check again under the
> page-table lock before insertion, because another thread can apply
> protection after collection finishes.
> 
> The alternative ...
> """
> 

Thanks, I'll trim it along these lines.

> I have concerns with this:
> 
>> The alternative would be to carry the uffd state over in
>> migrate_vma_insert_page() and migrate_vma_insert_huge_pmd_page(). Not
>> migrating the zero page is simpler, and a zero page that is protected by
>> userfaultfd in a range that a driver migrates should be rare.
> 
> This basically force-fails a migration of a uffd protected zero page.
> 
> Wouldn't we expect this to cause drivers to fail to make progress if
> they require that page to be resident on the device?  Not sure we want
> to force drivers to try to handle this special case (if they even can).
> 
> We'll need some input from the migrate_device/GPU folks.
>

for this is a little tricky without the right devices... I might drop this

Last comment 2026-11-13 Re: [RFC v2 1/1] mm: add source selector to memory.reclaim Neutral LLM

Adds source selector to memory.reclaim interface, enabling user-space control over memcg memory tiers.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Gregory Price (Meta) Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about the patch's motivation and interface flexibility. Suggested that user-space wants to shape memory distribution for performance, fairness, and cost reasons. Provided an example of how source-bound reclaim can be used in mixed DRAM/CXL systems.
Signals: NEEDS_WORK
Li Zhe ↳ replying to Gregory Price (Meta) Positive LLM
Acknowledged Gregory's concerns and provided additional context on the patch's motivation. Clarified that the goal is to enable user-space control over memcg memory tiers for performance, fairness, and cost reasons.
Show original comment 
On Fri, 13 Nov 2026 02:20:20 -0500, gourry@gourry.net wrote:

> > On 2026-11-02 15:04 +0800, Li Zhe wrote:
> > +	Specifying a source value constrains where proactive reclaim applies
> > +	pressure. Currently only source=nodes:<nodelist> is supported. For
> > +	example::
> > +
> > +	  echo "1G source=nodes:0-1" > memory.reclaim
> > +
> > +	This reclaims memory of the target cgroup resident on nodes 0-1.
> 
> you buried the fact that you want to allow source=zswap in your cover
> letter. I was confused by nodes: was needed here until i re-read.
> 
> As Joshua mentioned, i don't think you need the separate cover letter,
> and I agree this is far too long for the proposed change.

Agreed. The nodes: prefix was meant to keep source= a typed selector,
but I made the motivation and possible future extensions too long and
distracting. I will make v3 much clearer and shorter, and keep the focus
on source=nodes.

> ---
> 
> When I asked for testing, I wasn't concerned with the functionality,
> I was asking what problem this actually solves. performance? fairness?
> 
> Shaping the distribution of memory is neat and clearly works, but why
> does userspace want this?
> 
> The interface "lacking flexibility" is not a problem in and of itself.

Thanks, this is a fair point. Sorry, I also made that earlier
explanation too long, so let me summarize the intended use cases here:

https://lore.kernel.org/all/67fcd8db-269d-43f1-aea2-8c41165747d0@bytedance.com/

In short, performance, fairness and cost are all part of the goal. One
target is mixed DRAM/CXL systems where user space keeps each memcg near
its own DRAM/CXL resident ratio target, based on the workload's
latency/throughput and cost requirements. Another target is the case you
described below, where user space wants memcg-scoped reclaim to apply
pressure to the desired tier first, rather than reclaiming from warmer
memory on the top tier. This is especially useful when a memcg is close
to its memory limit and user space wants charge-reducing reclaim to
start from colder/lower-tier memory first.

> I CAN see why you might want to proactively shape what data is swapped.
> Let me give you an example:
> 
> ---
> The global node reclaim interfaces allow us to put pressure on distinct
> nodes - which lets us shape which node is swapped from first.
> 
> Example: node0-1=DRAM, node2-3:CXL
> 
>   echo 1G > node2/reclaim   # swap from CXL
>   echo 1G > node3/reclaim   # swap from CXL
>   echo 1G > node0/reclaim   # demote to CXL
>   echo 1G > node1/reclaim   # demote to CXL
> 
> But the memcg.reclaim interface only allows us to pressure the complete
> set of cpuset.mems nodes for that memcg
> 
>   cat cpuset.mems           # 0-3
>   echo 1G > memory.reclaim  # makes progress from any of 0-3
> 
> Presently, proactive reclaim iterates nodes in node or fallback order,
> meaning it attempts to make progress from the fastest node possible
> first - which attempts to demote by default.  If the lower nodes are
> full, this demotion will fail and we'll swap directly from the top tier,
> which is (presumably) warmer than the bottom tiers.
> 
> Enable source-bound reclaim in the memory.reclaim interface so that user
> space can have the same control over memcg memory tiers that global
> proactive reclaim has.
> 
>   echo 2G source=nodes:2-3 > memory.reclaim  # [z]swap from CXL
>   echo 2G source=nodes:0-1 > memory.reclaim  # demote to CXL
> 
> This lets user proactive reclaim to target the coldest memory first
> rather than preferring to reclaim from node0 first and potentially
> swap out memory warmer than what is present on a CXL tier.
> ---
> 
> With this, you might be able to demonstrate fewer refaults and less
> DRAM (node0) memory consumed by zswap. These are clear benefits.

Thanks for the example. It makes the problem much easier to discuss. I
will refer to it when reworking the v3 motivation, and will avoid
framing the issue as a generic flexibility problem.

> I would argue that memory.reclaim should operate this way anyway,
> and that would could make proactive reclaim do this by default, but
> I could see the policy bits in userland being useful as well
> 
> Performance may not be the only reason you want this, but you need to
> help me understand what you're trying to accomplish.

Thanks again for the review and suggestions.

Thanks,
Zhe
Signals: POSITIVE
Re: [PATCH v5 7/7] mm: use VMA flag helpers in NUMA balancing Neutral LLM

This patch introduces a new NUMA hinting scan mode called 'promotion-only' that allows the kernel to focus on identifying when VMAs become active, rather than optimizing scanning activity based on heuristics like read-only VMAs or VMA activeness. The patch adds a new flag MM_CP_PROT_NUMA_PROMO_ONLY and modifies various functions in the mm module to use this flag to determine the type of walk to perform. This change is necessary for memory-tiering fixes and improves the interface by keeping it focused on scan intent, rather than plumbing VMA-derived state.

Re: [PATCH 20/22] PM: hibernate: size the image from common memory Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Re: [PATCH v5 5/7] sched/numa: scan PID-inactive VMAs for promotion Neutral LLM

This patch introduces a new NUMA hinting scan mode called 'promotion-only' that allows the kernel to focus on identifying when VMAs become active, rather than optimizing scanning activity based on heuristics like read-only VMAs or VMA activeness. The patch adds a new flag MM_CP_PROT_NUMA_PROMO_ONLY and modifies various functions in the mm module to use this flag to determine the type of walk to perform. This change is necessary for memory-tiering fixes and improves the interface by keeping it focused on scan intent, rather than plumbing VMA-derived state.

\u5728 11/10/26 20:45, Gregory Price (Meta) \u5199\u9053:
> On 2026-11-10 14:05 +0800, Hongfu Li wrote:
>> From: Hongfu Li <lihongfu@kylinos.cn>
>>
>> alloc_hugetlb_folio_reserve() hands out a folio from the hugetlb pool and
>> consumes a global reservation, but unlike the fault path it never charged
>> the folio to the hugetlb cgroup or to the memcg.  Folios allocated for
>> memfd_pin_folios() through memfd_alloc_folio() were therefore silently
>> bypassing the accounting: they are never charged to the hugetlb cgroup
>> usage counter (hugetlb.<size>.current or hugetlb.<size>.usage_in_bytes),
>> so the limits (hugetlb.<size>.max or hugetlb.<size>.limit_in_bytes) are
>> bypassed.  In cgroup v2, with memory_hugetlb_accounting enabled, these
>> folios are also not accounted in memory.current and are not bounded by
>> memory.max.
>>
>> Charge the folios to both the hugetlb cgroup and the memcg, just like
>> hugetlb_alloc_folio() does.
>>
>> Test with a process that creates a hugetlb memfd and pins pages through
>> UDMABUF_CREATE. The counters were read while the folio was still pinned:
>>
> ...
>> Fixes: 89c1905d9c14 ("mm/gup: introduce memfd_pin_folios() for pinning memfd folios")
>> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
>> Signed-off-by: Hongfu Li <lihongfu@kylinos.cn>
> Reviewed-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
> Tested-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
>
> one note below that is worth assessing but maybe not a blocker.
>
>> +		ret = mem_cgroup_charge_hugetlb(folio, current->mm,
>> +						gfp_mask | __GFP_RETRY_MAYFAIL);
>> +		if (ret == -ENOMEM) {
>> +			folio_put(folio);
>> +			/*
>> +			 * Skip uncharging hugetlb_cgroup since the charges
>> +			 * were committed to the folio and freeing the folio
>> +			 * would have cleared those up.
>> +			 */
>> +			return NULL;
>> +		}
> This adds a new -ENOMEM condition on stable which may race subtly on
> two tasks allocating against the same memory.max and cause failures.
> In testing, it doesn't cause splats or anything, it may just cause
> spurious allocation failures that fallback to other paths.
>
> This implication may be worth a comment in the changelog.

Hi Gregory,

Thanks for the review and the testing.

Agreed on the new -ENOMEM condition. It is a real behaviour change that
affects stable kernels. I will add the following to the commit message
in v3:

memfd_pin_folios() can now fail because the memcg charge fails. Two
tasks allocating concurrently against the same memory.max may see a
spurious failure. That failure frees the folio, returns the global
reservation, and undoes the hugetlb cgroup charge, so no accounting is
left behind.

>
> ~Gregory
>

Best regards,
Hongfu

Jeff Layton Neutral LLM
Clarified the inconsistent behavior of i_version updates in btrfs, noting that it's a mix of suppressing and incrementing i_version under FMODE_NOCMTIME. Jeff also mentioned that this inconsistency is harmless for nfsd but might be worth addressing for neatness or future use cases.
Show original comment 
On Mon, 2026-11-09 at 17:10 +0100, David Sterba wrote:
> On Thu, Nov 05, 2026 at 03:07:41PM -0500, Jeff Layton wrote:
> > I tested the delegated timestamp support in nfsd recently. That support
> > relies heavily on FMODE_NOCMTIME, and I found several places where btrfs
> > didn't handle that flag properly. This series seems to fix them for me.
> > I tested mostly with the git regression testsuite, because it is very
> > sensitive to subtle bugs in timestamp handling.
> > 
> > Please consider for v7.5.
> > 
> > Signed-off-by: Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>
> > ---
> > Jeff Layton (4):
> >       btrfs: honor FMODE_NOCMTIME in btrfs_write_check()
> >       btrfs: honor FMODE_NOCMTIME when punching holes
> >       btrfs: honor FMODE_NOCMTIME in fallocate
> >       btrfs: honor FMODE_NOCMTIME when cloning
> 
> There's a comment from sashiko for patch 4 regarding the unconditional
> i_version update but this is mentioned in patch 3 as intentional. I
> can't tell if it's a problem, it seems more like a suggestion for
> cleanup. I'll add the series to for-next, please check if the question
> is valid. Thanks.

Thanks, I think the suggestion from sashiko is a false positive and we
should just merge this set as-is. That said, the kernel _is_
inconsistent in how it treats i_version under FMODE_NOCMTIME:

The generic VFS layer (so ext4, tmpfs, etc.) suppresses i_version
updates when FMODE_NOCMTIME is set.

XFS keeps incrementing it. This is because i_version behavior is a bit
different there (historical reasons). IIRC they more or less keep it in
lockstep with the LSN.

btrfs is mixed -- the write path suppresses it (like the generic VFS
does) but hole punching, fallocate, clone, etc. bump i_version anyway.
This is probably accidental.

In practice, it's harmless for nfsd. While there is a delegation
outstanding, it doesn't consult i_version anyway, and i_version is
updated when the delegation is released.

It might be worth making this more consistent at some point just for
neatness sake though, or in case we want to use i_version some other
way. I'll think about it.
-- 
Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>
Signals: WAITING_FOR_REVIEW

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-06 ⏱ No comments yet [PATCH v3 0/2] scsi: mpt3sas: Fix delayed not-responding sdev block handling 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] scsi: mpt3sas: Serialize interrupt-time sdev block with hostdata teardown Submitted 2026-11-06
  • [2/2] scsi: mpt3sas: Time out delayed not-responding device blocks Submitted 2026-11-06
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a deadlock issue in the mpt3sas SCSI driver, which occurs when handling delayed not-responding devices (dnr). The problem is caused by a race condition between interrupt-time blocking and timed unblocking of sdevs. To fix this, the patches introduce serialization mechanisms to prevent concurrent access to shared data structures, including the use of locks and delayed work queues. Specifically, the patches modify the scsih_sdev_init() function to publish sdev->hostdata under a lock, clear block_deadline on all affected sdevs before TUR polling starts, and take the ioc->block_lock in _scsih_block_io_all_device(). Additionally, dnr_unblock_work is deferred while scsi_host_in_recovery(), and mod_delayed_work() is used for retrying. The patches aim to break the deadlock by unblocking sdevs after a timeout, ensuring that the driver can recover from delayed not-responding devices without causing further issues.

Patches Submitted (4)
[PATCH v1] mm/rmap: fix migration entries being treated as partial unmaps Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a bug in the Linux kernel's memory management system where migration entries are being treated as partial unmaps, leading to incorrect behavior and statistics. When migrating or splitting large anonymous folios mapped by PTEs, the kernel incorrectly marks them as partially mapped, causing issues with deferred splits, statistics, and folio splitting. The patch introduces a flag to prevent this incorrect marking for migration entries, ensuring that only actual unmappings are treated as such.

[PATCH v2 0/4] mm: read root memcg stats from global counters instead of flushing 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] mm: zswap: fix shrinker count for the root memcg
  • [2/4] mm: memcg: read root lruvec stats from the node counters
  • [3/4] mm: workingset: skip the root memcg flush in workingset_test_recent()
  • [4/4] mm/damon/core: use node counters instead of flushing the root memcg
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch set improves performance by allowing the root memory cgroup (memcg) to read global counters directly instead of flushing its stats, which is expensive and can be stale. This change affects various kernel components, including zswap, LRU sizes, workingset, rotation, and reclaim writeback counts. The patches also make adjustments for specific use cases where reading global counters isn't sufficient or would introduce additional complexity. Overall, the goal is to reduce overhead and improve accuracy of memory cgroup statistics.

[PATCH v3 0/4] mm: read root memcg stats from global counters instead of flushing 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] mm: zswap: fix shrinker count for the root memcg
  • [2/4] mm: memcg: read root lruvec stats from the node counters
  • [3/4] mm: workingset: skip the root memcg flush in workingset_test_recent()
  • [4/4] mm/damon/core: use node counters instead of flushing the root memcg
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series modifies how memory cgroup (memcg) statistics are read from the root cgroup, which is a critical component in Linux's memory management system. Currently, reading memcg stats for the root cgroup involves flushing all other cgroups' stats, which can be expensive and introduce latency. This patch proposes to instead use global counters that track memory usage across all cgroups, eliminating the need for these flushes. The changes are incremental, with some call sites already using global counters, while others will require additional patches to adapt. The goal is to reduce overhead and improve performance in scenarios where the root cgroup's stats are accessed frequently.

[PATCH v2] mm/page_alloc: only skip kswapd nodes below their kswapd watermark Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the page allocation logic to only skip nodes with active kswapd if they are also below their target watermark. This change addresses a problem where high-order requests would wake up kswapd, causing it to stay awake and allocate pages from other nodes even when there was plenty of free memory on the original node. The patch aims to prevent this thrashing by only skipping nodes that are both under kswapd's control and have insufficient memory. This change is based on observations of Meta production workloads running on 2-NUMA systems, where processes would continuously allocate pages from a preferred node despite having hundreds of GB of free memory. The patch has been tested to show improved allocation behavior, with 100% of pages being served from the preferred node after applying the fix.

[PATCH v3] mm/page_alloc: only skip kswapd nodes below their kswapd watermark Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the behavior of get_page_from_freelist() to only skip nodes with kswapd running if they are below their relevant low-memory watermark. This change addresses a problem where high-order allocation failures would wake up kswapd and direct order-0 allocations to the fallback path, even when there is free space on the node. The patch was tested on 2-NUMA Meta production workloads and showed improved performance by reducing unnecessary pressure on remote nodes. The change only affects nodes that are under their low-memory watermark, ensuring that kswapd is not unnecessarily woken up.

  • [7/7] mm/memcontrol: restrict memory stock to leaf cgroups on v2 Submitted 2026-11-04
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series moves the stock implementation from memcontrol to page_counter, allowing for better separation of charge caching and policy management. The stock is a per-CPU cache of precharged pages that speeds up small allocations by avoiding frequent walks of the page_counter hierarchy. By moving it to page_counter, the patch simplifies the handling of stock in non-leaf cgroups and prepares for future improvements. It also ensures that locking behavior remains unchanged and that exact charging is preserved for non-spinning callers.

Last comment 2026-11-10 Re: [EXT.MSG] Re: [PATCH RFC bpf-next 0/2] bpf: Do not let preemption cause false recursion drops in trampoline progs Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses the issue of false recursion drops in trampoline programs caused by preemption. It introduces per-task, per-program tracking to replace the existing per-cpu recursion guard. The patch gives bpf_run_ctx a pointer to its outer ctx and associated bpf_prog, allowing it to traverse the nesting chain at trampoline entry. This approach reduces cross-task misses and improves performance, as demonstrated by test results showing a significant reduction in recursion misses.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Mike Bazov ↳ replying to JP (CrowdStrike) Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Mike Bazov noted that the patch is duplicating work from his own proposed design for v2, which covers additional use cases beyond trampolines and includes changes to handle concurrent execution of programs on the same CPU. He also pointed out specific issues with the patch's implementation, including a potential NULL dereference in bpf_io_uring_submit_sqes() and unnecessary cross-task synchronization.
Show original comment 
I haven't proposed moving away from an in-kernel solution. Your patch
is the same design I proposed earlier in this thread ("This is what I
have in mind for v2") with Andrii's bpf_ctx suggestion folded in, and
it's what I've been putting together for v2 (unless someone comes up
with a better direction), which is nearly ready to
post, so let's avoid duplicating the work.

beyond trampoline progs, v2 also covers raw_tp, tp_btf, syscall
tracepoints, and BPF_PROG_TEST_RUN, and disables the per-CPU private
stack for preemptible tracing progs since two tasks can now run the
same prog concurrently on one CPU.

a couple of notes on your patch:

- Passing prog to bpf_set_run_ctx() churns all callers that don't do
  recursion checks, and doesn't fit bpf_prog_run_array_sleepable(),
  which reuses one bpf_run_ctx across all progs in the array. In v2,
  bpf_set_run_ctx() keeps its signature and initializes prog to NULL,
  and bpf_prog_enter_recur_ctx() sets it after the walk succeeds.
- bpf_io_uring_submit_sqes() calls bpf_set_run_ctx(NULL), so
  dereferencing new_ctx unconditionally will NULL-deref there.
- Cross-task synchronization isn't needed: a task only ever walks its
  own current->bpf_ctx chain (and any IRQ/NMI on top of it runs on the
  same CPU and walks the same chain), so compiler barriers around
  linking and unlinking the frame are sufficient.

I'll Cc you on v2 once I post it (probably by tomorrow evening UTC).
testing and review there would be appreciated.

Mike

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-05 Last comment 2026-11-09 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2 0/2] arm64: sdei: Handle exceptions taken inside the event handler 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] arm64: sdei: Run the event handler on the kernel vectors Neutral Submitted 2026-11-05Last comment 2026-11-09 Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Catalin Marinas Ada Diaz
  • [2/2] arm64: sdei: Preserve the interrupted context's exception registers Neutral Submitted 2026-11-05Last comment 2026-11-06 Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Breno Leitao
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses an issue where SDEI events can cause exceptions in the event handler, leading to unexpected behavior and crashes. The patches modify the SDEI event handling code to run the handler on kernel vectors instead of trampoline vectors, which are not suitable for synchronous exceptions. Additionally, the patches save and restore exception registers around the event handler to prevent overwriting of interrupted context's registers. This change improves the robustness of SDEI event handling and prevents issues such as CPU stalls, SIGILL, and SIGSEGV in user tasks.

10 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Positive LLM
The author acknowledged that the IRQ-like resume into user space must still go through the trampoline to map the kernel, and confirmed that the patch series addresses the issue of exceptions taken by the handler breaking the interrupted context.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

An SDEI event can land anywhere, and a synchronous exception taken by its
handler breaks the context it interrupted:

 - With KPTI, an event that lands in user space runs the handler on the
   trampoline vectors, whose current-EL half is empty. The exception
   loops there and the CPU is lost.

 - The exception overwrites ELR, SPSR, ESR and FAR. If the event landed
   in exception entry or return, the interrupted context runs on the
   handler's values: a task gets SIGILL, or the kernel oopses on a fault
   it never took.

Patch 1 runs the handler on the kernel vectors. Patch 2 saves and
restores the exception registers around it.

Handlers take such exceptions in normal operation now that SDEI delivers
NMI backtraces: show_regs() reads through _nofault() accessors that
fault, and a kprobe or WARN anywhere in the path takes a BRK.

Tested under QEMU TCG with TF-A, 4 CPUs, 20 x sysrq-l under load, with a
test-only copy_from_kernel_nofault() of address 0x10 in
sdei_nmi_handler():

 - v7.4-rc1, KPTI on: CPUs stuck in the trampoline vectors, RCU stalls.
 - v7.4-rc1, KPTI off: 60 "unsafe: exception during handler" warnings,
   SIGILL and SIGSEGV in user tasks, and an oops in execve().
 - With the series, KPTI on and off: no warnings, no stalls. The same
   with an eprobe on irq_disable that reads address 0x10, which faults
   inside irqentry_nmi_enter() when the event lands in user space.

An earlier revision of patch 2 was also run on arm64 hardware with
firmware SDEI, where a plain sysrq-l was enough to trigger the warning
without it.

Changes since v1
================

  https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261104180936.2981070-1-kas@kernel.org/

 - Switch VBAR and save the exception registers in __sdei_handler(),
   before irqentry_nmi_enter(), and restore them after
   irqentry_nmi_exit(). Both can take an exception too: the eprobe above
   wedged v1 with KPTI on (Usama). do_sdei_event() takes the interrupted
   VBAR for the IRQ-like resume.

Signals: acknowledged fix, confirmed resolution
Breno Leitao Tested-by Neutral LLM
Breno Leitao tested the patch on NVIDIA Grace with firmware SDEI, KASAN + lockdep, and reported that without the series, the system crashed due to a task being interrupted in el0t_64_sync getting the handler's BRK, but with the series, no warnings or stalls were observed even under heavy stress.
Show original comment 
Tested-by: Breno Leitao <leitao@debian.org>

Tested on NVIDIA Grace (firmware SDEI) with next-20261104, KASAN + lockdep,
ARM_SDEI_NMI=y: sysrq-l under stress-ng, with a kprobe on show_regs() and
eprobes on irq_disable/irq_enable that fault on purpose.

Without the series the box crashed (a task interrupted in el0t_64_sync
got the handler's BRK, then lockdep/KASAN fallout and a hard lockup)

With it, KPTI off and kpti=1, ~4000 kprobe hits and ~5500 faults in
irqentry_nmi_enter()/exit() produced no warnings or stalls.

Catalin Marinas Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that irqs_disabled() may be false in __this_cpu_read() due to preempt debugging and pseudo-NMIs, which would cause a warning, and suggested using raw_cpu_read() or saving/restore PMR and priorities similar to kernel_entry code.
Show original comment 
Sashiko had a comment that the above should be raw_cpu_read() since with
preempt debugging and pseudo-NMIs, irqs_disabled() is false here and the
check in __this_cpu_read() will warn.

Thinking about it, further down on the irqentry_nmi_enter() ->
lockdep_hardirqs_off() we'll hit the irqs_disabled() issue again. We
could just do a raw_local_irq_save/restore() around this function.
Unnecessary for DAIF but useful with pseudo-NMIs.

Alternatively, __sdei_asm_handler() could save/restore PMR and change
the priorities similar to the kernel_entry code. Actually, this may be
better since do_sdei_event() also checks regs_irqs_disabled() and we
don't have any regs->pmr populated.

Worth a separate patch to fix SDEI handling with pseudo-NMI on and no
changes to __this_cpu_read() above needed. So, for this patch:

Reviewed-by: Catalin Marinas <catalin.marinas@arm.com>

(I'll probably queue it for -rc but waiting to see if there are more
comments)

Catalin

View on lore ↗
Ada Diaz Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Ada Diaz noted that the patch does not handle the case where an exception is taken inside the event handler, which can lead to a lost CPU and incorrect exception handling. Reviewer Ada Diaz suggested that the issue is not with the SDEI handler but rather with arch/arm64/entry.S not properly handling SDEI events, and also pointed out that the SDEI trampoline may be missing some kernel entry logic which could break assumptions. Reviewer Ada Diaz suggested that SDEI exception handling should be unified with regular kernel entry by swapping VBAR in __sdei_asm_entry_trampoline and __sdei_asm_exit_trampoline, rather than doing special handling in the handler. Reviewer Ada Diaz suggested modifying do_sdei_event() to explicitly choose the correct vector base for KPTI=on, bypassing unmapped EL1 exceptions of the trampoline vector by using vectors instead of VBAR. Reviewer Ada Diaz suggested replacing the code in __sdei_handler() with changes from entry.S, without modifying the existing function. Reviewer Ada Diaz suggested making the vector base on return from firmware more explicit, citing consistency with other entry paths and potential for future de-duplication.
Show original comment 
Hello,

On 05/11/2026 12:08, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:

---

This quite broken indeed, thank you for sending a patch !

However, given this and Catalin's comment regarding PMR save/restore,
to me this sounds more like `arch/arm64/entry.S` is not doing its job
properly for SDEI events, rather than the SDEI handler missing something.

In fact, it appears that the SDEI trampoline misses a fair amount of
the "regular" kernel and trampoline entry in general, which might subtly
break more assumptions...

---

Given that this is done in `entry.S` for a regular kernel entry,
I feel like this should be done there too for SDEI : bringing the two
paths closer rather than needing to do some additional special handling
in the handler.

`__sdei_asm_entry_trampoline` and `__sdei_asm_exit_trampoline` already
check if the CPU was running at EL0 and maps/unmaps the kernel accordingly.
We could swap VBAR to the EL1 vectors and back to the EL0 trampoline there...

---

...and to maintain the expected behaviouron return, rather than using
VBAR (which would bypass the trampoline in the EL0 IRQ-like resume case),
we could explicitly choose the correct vector base for KPTI=on ?
Something along those lines in `do_sdei_event()` :

	/* KPTI=off => vectors or the appropriate mitigation, KPTI=on => trampoline */
	unsigned long vbar = __raw_this_cpu_read(this_cpu_vector)
	[...]
	/* KPTI=on => have to bypass unmapped EL1 exceptions of the trampoline vector */
	if (arm64_kernel_unmapped_at_el0() && mode == kernel_mode)
		vbar = vectors
	[...]
	if (mode == kernel_mode)
		return vbar + 0x280;
	else if (mode & PSR_MODE32_BIT)
		return vbar + 0x680;

	return vbar + 0x480;

---

With what I mentioned above, this would be completely replaced by the
changes in `entry.S`, with no change in `__sdei_handler()`.

---

And this part would be made more explicit as to what vector base
we should be jumping back to on return from firmware.


What are your thoughts ?
I understand that this is a completely different approach, but I feel it
would be more in line with what we already do for other entry paths.
This might even allow us to de-duplicate them in the future and reduce
the chance of this happening again.

If my suggestion is not sound, apart from what Catalin already mentioned
the patch looks good to me !

Kind regards,
Ada

Reviewer Catalin Marinas pointed out that the patch does not handle the case where an exception is taken inside the event handler's interrupt handling code, which can lead to a deadlock between the per-vswap spinlock and the folio lock. Reviewer Catalin Marinas noted that the patch may have implications for VHE (EL2) and KVM world switch on hyp vectors, requesting further investigation before considering stable inclusion.

She requested that the patch be modified to save and restore the exception registers around the event handler. Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-04 Last comment 2026-11-14 [PATCH] mm/compaction: sample empty pageblocks again from a random offset Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the Linux kernel's compaction algorithm to sample empty pageblocks from a random offset when the initial scan finds nothing. This is done to prevent aliasing issues that can cause free pages to be missed, particularly in sparse layouts or when memory is not aligned with the sampling grid. The change aims to improve the detection of free pages and reduce the likelihood of compaction failures, which can lead to stalled tasks and inefficient memory usage.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Dev Jain ↳ replying to Kiryl (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Dev Jain questioned whether the patch's benefits would be observable in real workloads, citing low probabilities of missing free pages at random offsets and potential for unnecessary scans over fully filled memory.
Show original comment 
That is where it gets hairy: will this be observable in real workloads?

Suppose stride is 32 and the pageblock has 100 non-free pages. What is
the probability that 512 slots are filled with 100 objects such that
x = 0 (mod 32) is filled? It is

C(496, 84) / C(512, 100) = 1.6 *10^(-12), very improbable.

So the chance you have a sparse block and you are missing out by not
looking at other offsets is very low.

If stride = 32 and #objects = 400, the probability comes out to be
1.8%.

Keeping #objects fixed, lowering the stride lowers the probability
exponentially. (This is relevant since we start with lower strides)

On the other hand, what happens if you are scanning over fully filled
memory? (there is no way to tell whether a pageblock is sparse)
You are going to repeat the scans unnecessarily.

IOW I think the probability this patch is going to have a downside is
more than that of upside.
View on lore ↗
Signals: probability, downside

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-12 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v3] mm/memory: refuse to map page cache folios that are not uptodate Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch prevents mapping page cache folios that are not up-to-date, which can leak stale memory to userspace. It adds a new function `file_folio_not_uptodate` to check if a folio is uptodate and refuses the mapping in `__do_fault` and `do_set_pmd`. The fault gets a SIGBUS signal for non-uptodate folios, and MADV_COLLAPSE fails and leaves the range to be faulted in. This patch addresses a problem where some filesystems clear PG_uptodate on cached folios, which is considered buggy.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-02 Last comment 2026-11-13 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2 0/3] mm: make watermark checks honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: page_alloc: add a watermark check that knows the migratetype Neutral Submitted 2026-11-02Last comment 2026-11-13 Harry Yoo Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Vlastimil (SUSE)
  • [2/3] mm: page_alloc: make the high-order watermark check honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT Submitted 2026-11-02
  • [3/3] mm: vmscan: tell direct reclaim the allocation is ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT Neutral Submitted 2026-11-02Last comment 2026-11-13 Vlastimil (SUSE)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses a performance issue in Linux kernel's memory management, specifically when using defrag_mode and 64K mTHP (machine-specific transparent huge pages). The high-order watermark check was not honoring the ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT flag, leading to stalls in compaction and inefficient direct reclaim. The patches modify the watermark check to consider the request's migratetype and skip free lists that cannot be used by the request. Additionally, patch 3 informs direct reclaim about the allocation type. This change improves performance by reducing compaction stalls and increasing the efficiency of direct reclaim.

Kiryl Shutsemau (author) ↳ replying to Harry Yoo Neutral LLM
Author corrected the terminology used in the patch description to accurately reflect that ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT is a property of normal zone allocations, not defrag_mode, and provided an alternative version of the code. Show original comment 
It is ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT, not defrag_mode: Normal zone allocations get it
on the first attempt when DMA32 is populated, with defrag_mode off. And
such an allocation can still claim a whole free pageblock of another
migratetype.

But fair, it can be shorter. A tighter version is below; it also says

---

The value is not read there: __zone_watermark_ok_mt() uses the
migratetype only under ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT, and kcompactd never passes it.
kcompactd_node_suitable() makes the same call and has no compact_control
to take it from.

Thanks for the review.

The new changelog:

	The high-order check in __zone_watermark_ok() accepts a free page of any
	migratetype.  Under ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT, a request below pageblock_order can
	only take a page of its own migratetype or claim a whole free block, so
	the check can pass when the allocation cannot succeed.

	Only a check whether one allocation can be served now should count just
	the pages it can take.  A check whether a zone has enough free memory
	serves no single allocation and counts every migratetype: reclaim and
	compaction free whole pageblocks across migratetypes.

	Add __zone_watermark_ok_mt(), which takes the migratetype, for the first
	kind:

	  - the allocator fast path, zone_watermark_fast();
	  - its recheck after node reclaim in get_page_from_freelist();
	  - compaction's up-front check, compaction_suit_allocation_order().

	__zone_watermark_ok() wraps it for the second kind.  kcompactd shares
	compaction_suit_allocation_order() with direct compaction and passes
	MIGRATE_MOVABLE, which is not read without ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT.

	Declare both in mm/internal.h: nothing outside mm uses them.

	Nothing reads the migratetype yet: no functional change.  Preparation for
	making the high-order check honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT.

Looks good?

This patch modifies the behavior of page allocation in Linux's mm module to make defrag_mode retries follow the promoted order, rather than the original request order. Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series proposes a change to how Linux handles page cache reads, allowing multiple CPUs to read from the same folio without taking a reference. This is done by introducing a new flag PG_rcu_free and deferring the freeing of folios until an RCU grace period. The goal is to reduce contention between readers and improve performance in scenarios where many threads are reading the same pages. The patch series includes 18 patches that implement this change, including modifications to filemap_read(), shmem, and other related functions.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about memory safety when reading from the page cache without taking a reference, explaining that adding PG_rcu_free flag would allow readers to safely access folios marked for deferred free during an RCU grace period.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

Reading a page cache folio without taking a reference would keep CPUs
that read the same file from bouncing its refcount.  But without a
reference the folio can be freed, and its memory reused, while the
reader copies from it.

Add PG_rcu_free: the free of a folio with the flag waits for an RCU
grace period.  A reader that saw the flag under rcu_read_lock(), and
then still found the folio where it looked, can read it until
rcu_read_unlock().  A split copies the flag, as such a reader may still
be reading the new folios.

The folios wait on per-CPU lists, one grace period in flight per CPU:
call_rcu() per folio would queue a callback for every folio of a
truncated file.  A work, which can reschedule, frees each batch.
Reclaim depends on these frees, so the workqueue is WQ_MEM_RECLAIM.

The flag is 64-bit only (CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE): page flags are scarce
on 32-bit, and the contention this avoids is a problem of large
machines.  It is off on PREEMPT_RT, where expedited grace periods are
normal ones, so the waiting pages cannot be freed quickly when memory
runs short.

pgfree_rcu in /proc/vmstat counts the deferred pages.  Nothing sets the
flag yet.

Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: rework for flagging at read time, 64-bit only and not PREEMPT_RT,
 shorter comments and changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+config PAGE_RCU_FREE
+	def_bool y
+	depends on 64BIT
+	depends on !PREEMPT_RT
+	help
+	  Reads from the page cache can copy from folios that are read
+	  again and again without taking a reference on them, so small
+	  reads of one file from many CPUs do not contend on the folio's
+	  reference count.  The cost is that memory freed from such
+	  folios becomes reusable only after an RCU grace period.
+
+	  64-bit only: it takes a page flag, and those are scarce on
+	  32-bit.  Not on PREEMPT_RT, where expedited grace periods are
+	  normal ones, so freeing the waiting pages on demand would stall.
+

 	def_bool 64BIT

+/*
+ * Each CPU has at most one grace period in flight (@queued), covering
+ * @batch.  Folios freed meanwhile wait on @pending for the next one.
+ */
+struct rcu_free_pcpu {
+	spinlock_t lock;
+	bool queued;
+	struct list_head pending;
+	struct list_head batch;
+	struct rcu_work rwork;
+};
+
+static DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct rcu_free_pcpu, rcu_free_pcpu);
+
+static struct workqueue_struct *rcu_free_wq __ro_after_init;
+
+static void rcu_free_start(struct rcu_free_pcpu *r)
+{
+	lockdep_assert_held(&r->lock);
+
+	if (r->queued || list_empty(&r->pending))
+		return;
+
+	list_splice_init(&r->pending, &r->batch);
+	r->queued = true;
+	WARN_ON_ONCE(!queue_rcu_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->rwork));
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_pages(struct list_head *list)
+{
+	struct folio_batch fbatch;
+	struct folio *folio, *next;
+
+	folio_batch_init(&fbatch);
+	list_for_each_entry_safe(folio, next, list, lru) {
+		if (!folio_batch_add(&fbatch, folio)) {
+			free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
+			cond_resched_tasks_rcu_qs();
+		}
+	}
+	if (folio_batch_count(&fbatch))
+		free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_workfn(struct work_struct *work)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = container_of(to_rcu_work(work),
+					       struct rcu_free_pcpu, rwork);
+	LIST_HEAD(done);
+
+	spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+	list_splice_init(&r->batch, &done);
+	r->queued = false;
+	rcu_free_start(r);
+	spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+
+	rcu_free_pages(&done);
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_defer(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = raw_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu);
+	unsigned long flags;
+
+	spin_lock_irqsave(&r->lock, flags);
+	list_add_tail(&folio->lru, &r->pending);
+	rcu_free_start(r);
+	spin_unlock_irqrestore(&r->lock, flags);
+}
+
+static void __init rcu_free_init(void)
+{
+	int cpu;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return;
+
+	rcu_free_wq = alloc_workqueue("rcu_free",
+				      WQ_MEM_RECLAIM | WQ_PERCPU, 0);
+
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
+		struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu);
+
+		spin_lock_init(&r->lock);
+		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->pending);
+		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->batch);
+		INIT_RCU_WORK(&r->rwork, rcu_free_workfn);
+	}
+}
+
 static __always_inline bool __free_pages_prepare(struct page *page,
 		unsigned int order, fpi_t fpi_flags)
 {
@@ -1327,6 +1417,19 @@ static __always_inline bool __free_pages_prepare(struct page *page,
 
 	VM_BUG_ON_PAGE(PageTail(page), page);
 
+	/*
+	 * Not for a hwpoisoned order-0 page: it never reaches the allocator,
+	 * and page_handle_poison() takes a reference right after this free.
+	 */
+	if (unlikely(folio_test_rcu_free(folio)) &&
+	    !(PageHWPoison(page) && !order)) {
+		VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(fpi_flags & FPI_NOLOCK);
+		__folio_clear_rcu_free(folio);
+		count_vm_events(PGFREE_RCU, 1 << order);
+		rcu_free_defer(folio);
+		return false;
+	}
+
 	trace_mm_page_free(page, order);
 	kmsan_free_page(page, order);
 
@@ -6401,6 +6504,8 @@ void __init setup_per_cpu_pageset(void)
 	for_each_online_pgdat(pgdat)
 		pgdat->per_cpu_nodestats =
 			alloc_percpu(struct per_cpu_nodestat);

2.54.0

---

From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

PG_rcu_free holds back the reuse of a folio's memory until a grace
period after the folio is freed.  A stolen page cache pipe buffer is
reused without being freed: remove_mapping() takes the folio out of the
page cache and the caller keeps it.  fuse_try_move_folio() puts it into
a fuse file, where writes change it under a reader that found it before
the steal.

Drop ->try_steal from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops.  fuse_try_move_folio()
and virtio_console's pipe_to_sg() then copy, as they already do when
stealing fails.  SPLICE_F_MOVE from the page cache into a fuse file or
a virtio console now copies.

Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: rewrote the changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0

---

fuse_try_move_folio() takes the folio of a pipe buffer and puts it into
a fuse file, where writes change it.  A folio with PG_rcu_free may still
be copied by a lockless page cache read that found it before it left
the page cache.

Such a folio reaches fuse when a mapped file page is gifted with
vmsplice(SPLICE_F_GIFT) and its file is then truncated.
fuse_check_folio() refuses it only because the flag is not among those
it allows, and with a dump_page().

Copy such a folio instead, as fuse does whenever it cannot take one.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0

---

Deferred folios have refcount 0 and are not on the free lists, so no
counter shows them.  Memory that waits for a grace period is missing
from the free count and from every LRU count.

Count it per zone in NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES: nr_free_rcu in /proc/vmstat,
free_rcu in show_free_areas().  Preparation for freeing the waiting
pages sooner when they hold back memory that is needed.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern that the expedited grace period should only be triggered when memory is low, and explained how this is implemented by taking both CPU lists and freeing them after an expedited grace period when more than 4 MiB is pending on a CPU. The author also clarified that the expedited grace period interrupts every CPU and should be used sparingly.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

A deferred folio waits one to two grace periods on its CPU's list, where
the page allocator cannot use it.  A CPU that frees many flagged folios
at once, for example when a file is truncated, can hold a lot of memory
there.

When more than 4 MiB is pending on a CPU, a work takes both of the CPU's
lists and frees them after an expedited grace period.  That grace period
starts after the take, so it covers every reader that found one of the
folios.  The task that frees them does not wait.

An expedited grace period interrupts every CPU, so keep it for when the
memory matters.  That is when the zone is below its high watermark, or
when the pages waiting in it are more than an eighth of its free pages.
The watermark alone comes too late for a bulk free.  A truncate or
fadvise(POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED) of a hot range defers all of it while the
zone still has memory.  When the readers that refill the range then run
short, nothing is freed that would check again.

With rcu_normal, the expedited grace period is a normal one.

pgfree_rcu_expedite in /proc/vmstat counts the expedite runs that freed
something.

Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: take both lists, expedite only when the memory matters, rewrote
 the changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* Free what rcu_free_take() took: the grace period starts after that */
+static unsigned long rcu_free_flush(struct list_head *list)

+static void rcu_free_expedite_workfn(struct work_struct *work)

+	/* Unlocked: a stale count delays the expedite by one free */
+	if (data_race(r->nr_pending) <= RCU_FREE_EXPEDITE ||
+	    work_pending(&r->expedite))
+		return;
+
+	/*
+	 * An expedited grace period interrupts every CPU: only while the
+	 * zone is short of memory, or while more than an eighth of what it
+	 * has free waits here.
+	 */
+	free = zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_PAGES);
+	waiting = zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);
+	if (free < high_wmark_pages(zone) || waiting > free / 8)
+		queue_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->expedite);
 }

-- 
2.54.0

---

When reclaim fails, should_reclaim_retry() looks only at free and
reclaimable pages.  It can hand the allocation to the OOM killer while
enough memory is about to come back from the pages that wait for a
grace period.

Before should_reclaim_retry() gives up, free the waiting pages if they
and what reclaim can still free would bring an eligible zone above its
min watermark, and retry.  The drain frees them straight to the buddy
lists, where the retry's watermark check sees them, and waits for the
expedites already in flight.  That costs an expedited grace period,
which is cheap next to the OOM killer.  The retries are bounded per
no-progress streak, so a drain that does not help still ends in an OOM
kill.

pgfree_rcu_drain in /proc/vmstat counts the drains that freed something.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/*
+ * Free what every CPU's lists hold, straight to the buddy lists: the
+ * callers want to allocate the pages next.  A concurrent drain holds the
+ * lock until it has freed what it took.
+ */
+static void rcu_free_drain(void)
+{
+	static DEFINE_MUTEX(lock);
+	LIST_HEAD(list);
+	int cpu;
+
+	mutex_lock(&lock);
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
+		struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu);
+
+		spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+		rcu_free_take(r, &list);
+		spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+	}
+	if (rcu_free_flush(&list, true))
+		count_vm_event(PGFREE_RCU_DRAIN);
+	/* An expedite in flight holds pages it took off the lists */
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu)
+		flush_work(&per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu)->expedite);
+	mutex_unlock(&lock);
+}

+/*
+ * Reclaim is about to give up: if the pages waiting for a grace period,
+ * with what reclaim can still free, would bring an eligible zone above its
+ * min watermark, free them and retry.  Not for a dying task, which frees
+ * memory by exiting.
+ */
+static bool rcu_free_wait(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order,
+			  struct alloc_context *ac, int alloc_flags,
+			  int *rcu_free_waits)
+{
+	struct zone *zone;
+	struct zoneref *z;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return false;
+	if (tsk_is_oom_victim(current) || fatal_signal_pending(current))
+		return false;
+	if (*rcu_free_waits >= MAX_RECLAIM_RETRIES ||
+	    order > PAGE_ALLOC_COSTLY_ORDER)
+		return false;
+
+	for_each_zone_zonelist_nodemask(zone, z, ac->zonelist,
+				ac->highest_zoneidx, ac->nodemask) {
+		unsigned long waiting, available;
+
+		if (cpusets_enabled() && (alloc_flags & ALLOC_CPUSET) &&
+		    !__cpuset_zone_allowed(zone, gfp_mask))
+			continue;
+
+		waiting = zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);
+		if (!waiting)
+			continue;
+
+		available = waiting + zone_reclaimable_pages(zone) +
+			    zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_PAGES);
+		/*
+		 * Count pages only: for order > 0 __zone_watermark_ok() also
+		 * wants a free block of that order, and the waiting pages are
+		 * not free yet.
+		 */
+		if (__zone_watermark_ok(zone, 0, min_wmark_pages(zone),
+					ac->highest_zoneidx, alloc_flags,
+					available)) {
+			(*rcu_free_waits)++;
+			rcu_free_drain();
+			return true;
+		}
+	}
+
+	return false;
+}

2.54.0

---

alloc_contig_range() and offline_pages() migrate the folios out of a
range and then check with test_pages_isolated() that every page is free.
A migrated folio that had PG_rcu_free waits for a grace period after its
free, so the check fails.  alloc_contig_range() returns -EBUSY, and a
CMA or gigantic page allocation can fail; offline_pages() retries until
the grace period ends.

When the check fails and the zone has pages waiting, free them first.
alloc_contig_range() then finds the start of the free range again, as
the freed folio can merge with a free buddy below it.
offline_pages() does so only after a pass that migrated folios: an
offline stuck on a busy page would otherwise start an expedited grace
period on every retry.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0

---

A block that compaction has migrated every folio out of becomes free
once those folios are back in the buddy lists.  A migrated folio that
had PG_rcu_free comes back only after a grace period, so compaction
finds the block still in use, migrates more blocks than it needs and
fails more often.  defrag_mode relies on compaction making whole free
blocks, and there it shows up as allocation stalls.

When compaction moves past a block it migrated from and the zone has
pages waiting, free them before it drains the per-cpu lists and checks
whether it is done.  The drain costs an expedited grace period, so
async compaction, which must not block, skips it.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed David Hildenbrand's concern about removing ->try_steal from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops, explaining that fuse_try_move_folio() and virtio_console's pipe_to_sg() already copy the stolen page cache pipe buffer when stealing fails. The author confirmed this change is a simplification and does not have any significant performance impact.
Show original comment 
A page whose free waits for an RCU grace period has refcount 0 and is
neither on the LRU nor in the buddy allocator.  memory_failure() and
soft_offline_page() cannot handle such a page: get_any_page() retries a
few times and gives up.  Soft offline of the page fails, and a hard
error is ignored.  If the page is part of a large folio, the folio is
later freed with the poisoned page in it, and that page can be handed
out again.

Free the waiting pages first when the page looks like one of them, and
again on each retry in get_any_page(): the page can be freed while it
retries.  memory_failure() has to do it before it sets PG_hwpoison.  The
free of a poisoned order-0 page drops the page instead of returning it
to the buddy allocator, so a later drain would still leave the error
ignored.

A folio with PG_rcu_free that is still in use may be copied by a reader
that holds no reference.  When memory_failure() succeeds, its caller can
unmap the page from the direct map under such a reader.  Wait for a
grace period before returning success.  By then the page has left the
page cache, or is an order-0 folio with PG_hwpoison, which a reader that
starts after the grace period has to check.

Wait at the end, not right after setting PG_hwpoison.  A large folio is
split later, and until the new folios are in the page cache, a reader
can still copy the poisoned page as part of the large one.

Both callers hold mf_mutex and can wait for the grace period.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* A page waiting for a grace period is not in the buddy yet: free it */
+static void mf_free_rcu_pages(struct page *p)
+{
+	if (!zone_rcu_free_waiting(page_zone(p)))
+		return;
+	if (page_count(p))
+		return;
+	if (is_free_buddy_page(p))
+		return;
+
+	rcu_free_sync();
+}

 		ret = __get_hwpoison_page(p, flags);
 		if (!ret) {
@@ -2440,6 +2454,7 @@ int memory_failure(unsigned long pfn, int flags)
 	if (res != -ENOENT)
 		goto unlock_mutex;
 
+	mf_free_rcu_pages(p);
 	if (TestSetPageHWPoison(p)) {
 		res = -EHWPOISON;
 		if (flags & MF_ACTION_REQUIRED)
@@ -2447,6 +2462,13 @@ int memory_failure(unsigned long pfn, int flags)
 		if (flags & MF_COUNT_INCREASED)
 			put_page(p);
 		action_result(pfn, MF_MSG_ALREADY_POISONED, MF_FAILED);

+		 * The memory_failure() that set PG_hwpoison may have failed
+		 * without waiting for lockless readers, and the caller may
+		 * unmap the page once this returns 0.

+	 * readers that started before the page got PG_hwpoison, or before a
+	 * split left it in a folio of its own, may still be copying it.

On Wed, Nov 04, 2026 at 05:05:29PM +0100, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> On 11/4/26 14:00, Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) wrote:
> > From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
> > 
> > PG_rcu_free holds back the reuse of a folio's memory until a grace
> > period after the folio is freed.  A stolen page cache pipe buffer is
> > reused without being freed: remove_mapping() takes the folio out of the
> > page cache and the caller keeps it.  fuse_try_move_folio() puts it into
> > a fuse file, where writes change it under a reader that found it before
> > the steal.
> > 
> > Drop ->try_steal from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops.  fuse_try_move_folio()
> > and virtio_console's pipe_to_sg() then copy, as they already do when
> > stealing fails.  SPLICE_F_MOVE from the page cache into a fuse file or
> > a virtio console now copies.
> > 
> > Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
> > [kas: rewrote the changelog]
> > Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
> > ---
> >  fs/splice.c | 51 ---------------------------------------------------
> 
> Removing code is always nice, even independent of the other changes :)
> 
> The description doesn't really describe the impact of this change. I assume a
> slowdown of certain workloads (does anybody really care?), and temporary
> increased memory consumption (as we keep the pagecache page).
> 
> No idea how often stealing actually succeeds ... a bit of history why it was
> added in the first place and why we no longer care now would be nice.

SPLICE_F_MOVE has not moved page cache folios between files since 2007.
See 485ddb4b9741 ("1/2 splice: dont steal").  The only users that still
steal from a pipe are fuse, when a server splices into /dev/fuse with
SPLICE_F_MOVE, and virtio_console's splice_write().  With this patch
both copy, and the source file keeps its folio cached.

Miklos, Bernd, Amit: do you know of users that depend on the move?  Would
the copy hurt them?

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Makes sense.  Something like the diff below, on top of this patch.
Introducing split_copy_flags would be a preparatory patch, and this one
would only add __PG_RCU_FREE to it.

> > @@ -1327,6 +1417,19 @@ static __always_inline bool __free_pages_prepare(struct page *page,
> >  
> >  	VM_BUG_ON_PAGE(PageTail(page), page);
> >  
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Not for a hwpoisoned order-0 page: it never reaches the allocator,
> > +	 * and page_handle_poison() takes a reference right after this free.
> > +	 */
> > +	if (unlikely(folio_test_rcu_free(folio)) &&
> > +	    !(PageHWPoison(page) && !order)) {
> > +		VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(fpi_flags & FPI_NOLOCK);
> > +		__folio_clear_rcu_free(folio);
> > +		count_vm_events(PGFREE_RCU, 1 << order);
> > +		rcu_free_defer(folio);
> > +		return false;
> > +	}
> I'm wondering whether we want to intercept on the folio freeing path instead?
> That would be __folio_put(), before we go into free_frozen_pages() used by other
> machinery that does not apply (slab etc).

Not all page cache folios are freed through __folio_put().  Reclaim
frees them with free_unref_folios() directly (shrink_folio_list(),
move_folios_to_lru()), and so do folios_put_refs() and the LRU batches.
__free_pages_prepare() is the one place they all pass.

David (Arm) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer David (Arm) noted that removing the ->try_steal function from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops could lead to a slowdown of certain workloads and temporary increased memory consumption, as the folio's memory is kept in the page cache until an RCU grace period. He also requested more information on why stealing was added in the first place and why it is no longer needed.

On 11/4/26 14:00, Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) wrote:
> From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
> 
> Reading a page cache folio without taking a reference would keep CPUs
> that read the same file from bouncing its refcount.  But without a
> reference the folio can be freed, and its memory reused, while the
> reader copies from it.
> 
> Add PG_rcu_free: the free of a folio with the flag waits for an RCU
> grace period.  A reader that saw the flag under rcu_read_lock(), and
> then still found the folio where it looked, can read it until
> rcu_read_unlock().  A split copies the flag, as such a reader may still
> be reading the new folios.
> 
> The folios wait on per-CPU lists, one grace period in flight per CPU:
> call_rcu() per folio would queue a callback for every folio of a
> truncated file.  A work, which can reschedule, frees each batch.
> Reclaim depends on these frees, so the workqueue is WQ_MEM_RECLAIM.
> 
> The flag is 64-bit only (CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE): page flags are scarce
> on 32-bit, and the contention this avoids is a problem of large
> machines.  It is off on PREEMPT_RT, where expedited grace periods are
> normal ones, so the waiting pages cannot be freed quickly when memory
> runs short.
> 
> pgfree_rcu in /proc/vmstat counts the deferred pages.  Nothing sets the
> flag yet.
> 
> Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
> [kas: rework for flagging at read time, 64-bit only and not PREEMPT_RT,
>  shorter comments and changelog]
> Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
> ---

> +/*
> + * Each CPU has at most one grace period in flight (@queued), covering
> + * @batch.  Folios freed meanwhile wait on @pending for the next one.
> + */
> +struct rcu_free_pcpu {
> +	spinlock_t lock;
> +	bool queued;
> +	struct list_head pending;
> +	struct list_head batch;
> +	struct rcu_work rwork;
> +};
> +
> +static DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct rcu_free_pcpu, rcu_free_pcpu);
> +
> +static struct workqueue_struct *rcu_free_wq __ro_after_init;
> +
> +static void rcu_free_start(struct rcu_free_pcpu *r)
> +{
> +	lockdep_assert_held(&r->lock);
> +
> +	if (r->queued || list_empty(&r->pending))
> +		return;
> +
> +	list_splice_init(&r->pending, &r->batch);
> +	r->queued = true;
> +	WARN_ON_ONCE(!queue_rcu_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->rwork));
> +}
> +
> +static void rcu_free_pages(struct list_head *list)
> +{
> +	struct folio_batch fbatch;
> +	struct folio *folio, *next;
> +
> +	folio_batch_init(&fbatch);
> +	list_for_each_entry_safe(folio, next, list, lru) {
> +		if (!folio_batch_add(&fbatch, folio)) {
> +			free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
> +			cond_resched_tasks_rcu_qs();
> +		}
> +	}
> +	if (folio_batch_count(&fbatch))
> +		free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
> +}
> +
> +static void rcu_free_workfn(struct work_struct *work)
> +{
> +	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = container_of(to_rcu_work(work),
> +					       struct rcu_free_pcpu, rwork);
> +	LIST_HEAD(done);
> +
> +	spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
> +	list_splice_init(&r->batch, &done);
> +	r->queued = false;
> +	rcu_free_start(r);
> +	spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
> +
> +	rcu_free_pages(&done);
> +}
> +
> +static void rcu_free_defer(struct folio *folio)
> +{
> +	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = raw_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu);
> +	unsigned long flags;
> +

> +

> +
> +		spin_lock_init(&r->lock);
> +		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->pending);
> +		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->batch);
> +		INIT_RCU_WORK(&r->rwork, rcu_free_workfn);
> +	}
> +}
