Linus Torvalds Inline Review Reviewed-by Contentious LLM
Reviewer Linus Torvalds expressed strong disagreement with Kiryl's patch, arguing that attempting to corral RCU freeing into a special case would make the code fragile and less useful, and instead suggested removing fuse_try_move_folio() entirely. He also proposed getting rid of pipe_buf_try_steal() and its associated functions.
Show original comment 
On Mon, 9 Nov 2026 at 06:43, David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org> wrote:
>
> Can we just rework that code first to have some clear folio freeing path, IOW
> doing this in a folio-specific helper?

I think you're making things worse rather than better.

The whole point was to hook into *one* place: the page freeing.

Trying to make it specific to one special freeing path only makes it
fragile and less obvious, and results in anybody who then uses some
other freeing model to miss the RCU thing.

It also means that suddenly nobody else can use the RCU path for their own uses.

Look at all the kmalloc RCU freeing: it's used all over the place. RCU
has basically taken over the kernel. Every single performance-critical
lock-free area of the kernel wants RCU freeing.

I think trying to corral it into some special case is the last thing
we want to do. It only makes things less potentially useful and more
fragile.

                  Linus

I think we sh ould just get rid of 'try_steal' entirely. I don't think
anybody really uses it. I think it exists in fuse and in the virtio

Removing pipe_buf_try_steal() would basically remove all of
fuse_try_move_folio().

                 Linus

---

On Mon, 9 Nov 2026 at 09:24, David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org> wrote:
> >
> > I think trying to corral it into some special case is the last thing
> > we want to do. It only makes things less potentially useful and more
> > fragile.
> Let me wrap my head around how this would look when we decouple pages from
> folios, where pages might not have a ->lru anymore, or not even a refcount.

So for an example of a place that already does RCU freeing of pages -
*not* folios - look at the TLB shootdown.

It's very round-about and hard to tell, but it's using

  tlb_remove_table_free
    call_rcu(&batch->rcu, tlb_remove_table_rcu);

and that then does

  tlb_remove_table_rcu ->
    __tlb_remove_table_free ->
      __tlb_remove_table ->
        pagetable_dtor_free ->
          pagetable_free ->
            __pagetable_free ->
               __free_pages(page, compound_order(page))

(and I might have messed this up - this really is a rats nest of
nested inline functions).

And in order to do this all, it sets up *another* page just to contain
the batching (which is also RCU-free'd)

Now, the batching is probably a very good thing in that context, so
I'm not actually suggesting this should be converted to some common
RCU-freeing thing.

Rather, I'm pointing out that we have existing hacks in this area that
have grown up over time and would have been a *lot* less hacky if we
had had a proper RCU-freeing page allocator back when that whole TLB
freeing code was written.

So I'm not saying "we should unify this". But I *am* saying "look, RCU
freeing of pages is _already_ a thing, and we have complicated hacky
things in place for it".

And they are complicated not because they necessarily should be
complicated, but because we solved the TLB shootdown after we already
had tons of complex architecture-specific code in place, and we
basically ended up adding that RCU phase on top of that existing
per-architecture complexity and abstraction.

*NOW* it would be actually fairly painful to try to convert the TLB
shootdown to a new order. But originally, it would have been very nice
if we had just had a "Oh, mark this page for RCU freeing".

(In all fairness, we also did it that way because some architectures
don't need the RCU delay simply because they have some other hardware
page walking infrastructure than the actual page tables the kernel
builds up. Not that those architectures *matter* today, but some of
that per-architecture inline function stuff is required to basically
isolate these kinds of decisions from the MM code - that deep inline
function indirection has a reason that is entirely independent of the
freeing side).

                    Linus

Last comment 2026-11-10 [PATCH 0/4] splice: remove pipe buffer stealing 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] virtio_console: copy pipe buffers in splice_write() Neutral Last comment 2026-11-10 Zi Yan Matthew Wilcox
  • [2/4] fuse: don't move pipe buffers into the page cache Neutral Last comment 2026-11-10 Zi Yan
  • [3/4] splice: remove pipe buffer stealing
  • [4/4] splice: replace nosteal_pipe_buf_ops with default_pipe_buf_ops
Neutral LLM

This patch series removes the pipe buffer stealing mechanism, which allows consumers to take pages from a pipe buffer instead of copying data. The mechanism is used by fuse and virtio_console, but it has been causing issues and is no longer necessary as both consumers can copy data when stealing fails. The patches replace the nosteal_pipe_buf_ops with default_pipe_buf_ops and remove the stealing mechanism.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-05 Last comment 2026-11-09 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2] x86/mm: Remove the zapped-entry checks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch removes the zapped-entry checks on x86 architecture, which were implemented to warn about shadow stack entries in VMAs without VM_SHADOW_STACK. The checks are only relevant when CONFIG_DEBUG_VM is enabled and the SRSO 'Safe RET' mitigation is active. Removing these checks improves performance by reducing branch mispredictions, resulting in a 6% speedup for MADV_DONTNEED of 1 GiB on anonymous memory and 8% on shmem, as well as a 5% improvement for munmap(). The patch also removes the pud_shstk() function, which had no other users.

Re: [PATCH] Documentation/userfaultfd: document RWP behavior when the PTE is dropped Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch updates the documentation of userfaultfd to accurately describe the behavior of Read-Write Protectors (RWP) when a Page Table Entry (PTE) is dropped. Specifically, it clarifies that RWP faults can occur on shmem and hugetlbfs mappings, not just anonymous pages, and that the kernel can drop PTEs at any time while the page remains in the page cache or swap. Additionally, it corrects comments related to UFFDIO_SET_MODE and UFFDIO_REGISTER to ensure accurate documentation of their behavior.

Re: [RFC PATCH v2 03/18] fuse: don't move a folio with PG_rcu_free into a fuse file Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Re: [RFC PATCH v2 08/18] mm/compaction: free the migrated folios that wait for a grace period Neutral LLM
Last comment 2026-11-09 Re: [PATCH v2] mm/huge_memory: prevent losing PG_waiters in __split_folio_to_order() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a bug in the __split_folio_to_order() function where PG_waiters could be lost when splitting a compound page. The problem arises from setting PG_head after clear_compound_head(), which breaks the ordering requirement that page flags must be visible before the page becomes non-compound. To fix this, the patch builds the whole flags word for the new head, including PG_head, and stores it once before smp_wmb(). This approach ensures that PG_waiters are preserved during the split operation.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer Zi Yan noted that prep_compound_page() writing PG_head after clear_compound_head() breaks the page flags visibility requirement, and suggested building the whole flags word for the new head, including PG_head, before smp_wmb(). Reviewer Zi Yan suggested using data_race() to handle the potential race condition when setting PG_head on new_folio, as the flags are stable at this moment. Reviewer Zi Yan noted that setting PG_head atomically on its own breaks the ordering requirement that page flags must be visible before the page becomes non-compound, and suggested building the whole flags word for the new head, including PG_head, and storing it once before smp_wmb().

Yes, thank you for the explanation. I confused myself when writing
the comment. At this code place, before clear_compound_head(),
reference-less accessors can only see the old folio.

>
> CPU1 and CPU2 below are two faults on a migration entry that points at
> new_head. Both take this path:
>
>   do_swap_page()
>     migration_entry_wait()
>       softleaf_entry_wait_on_locked()
>         softleaf_to_folio()
>           page_folio()           <-- old head or new_head?
>         folio_set_waiters()
>           set_bit(PG_waiters)    <-- on that folio's flags
>
> Which one page_folio() returns depends on clear_compound_head():
>
> CPU0: __split_folio_to_order()  CPU1: fault           CPU2: fault
>
>                                 1. page_folio()
>                                    -> old head
> 2. flags  = new_folio->flags.f
>    flags |= folio->flags.f
>                                 3. set_bit(PG_waiters)
>                                    -> folio->flags.f
> 4. new_folio->flags.f =
>            flags | PG_head
>    smp_wmb()
> 5. clear_compound_head()
>                                                       6. page_folio()
>                                                          -> new_head
>                                                       7. set_bit(PG_waiters)
>                                                          -> new_folio->flags.f
>
> 2 and 3 race on the old head's flags word.
>
> That is Ye Liu's KCSAN report, and it is benign: PG_waiters is not in
> the cloned mask. new_folio->flags.f gets PG_waiters only at 7, after
> CPU0's last plain write to it at 4.
>
> So only the folio->flags.f read needs data_race().
>
> The new_folio->flags.f read needs nothing; the READ_ONCE() I had there
> in my sketch was not needed.

Yes and I should have checked it more carefully, but my focus was mostly
on whether PG_head could be set before smp_wmb() and clear_compound_head().

Last comment 2026-11-09 Re: [PATCH v2 1/2] mm/memory: recheck PG_uptodate after ->page_mkwrite() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses a problem where page cache folios that are not up-to-date can be mapped, leading to stale memory being leaked to userspace. The patches add a backstop to refuse mapping such folios in the __do_fault() and do_set_pmd() functions. Patch 1 fixes a race condition that would trip over this backstop by rechecking PG_uptodate after ->page_mkwrite().

On Tue, Nov 10, 2026 at 10:38:34AM +0000, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> On Mon, Nov 09, 2026 at 08:30:47PM +0000, Matthew Wilcox wrote:
> > On Mon, Nov 09, 2026 at 12:04:01PM +0000, Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) wrote:
> > > do_shared_fault() unlocks the folio around ->page_mkwrite(). Writeback
> > > can lock it in that window, fail, and clear PG_uptodate with the folio
> > > left in the page cache. ecryptfs_writepages() does this when encryption
> > > fails. filemap_page_mkwrite() relocks the folio and checks only
> > > ->mapping, so the fault maps a folio that is not uptodate:
> > 
> > Filesystems that do this are buggy.  They shuld be fixed, not worked
> > around like this.

> 
> Is there a timeline for the filesystem fixes?
> 
> Until they land, mm should still not map a folio that is not uptodate.
> 
> Would WARN_ON_ONCE() and SIGBUS, like the other checks in 2/2, work for
> you? It flags the buggy filesystem instead of hiding it.

It doesn't matter which way round the race ends up; if a filesystem marks
a mapped folio as !uptodate, there are other VM assertions that will
trip eventually.  This is one of those cases where we should ignore the
bot because it doesn't quite know enough.

Patch proposes using REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM, providing an alternative to the existing implementation.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau Inline Review Neutral LLM
Raised questions about alignment requirements for RET instructions in replacement code, suggesting a possible solution to avoid ITS problems. Proposed an alternative layout with a swap of RCX and RDX registers.
Signals: NEEDS_WORK
David Laight ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Needs Work LLM
Expressed concerns about the patch's proposal, citing potential issues with alignment and performance. Suggested that a simple backtrack could improve register usage.
Show original comment 
On Tue, 10 Nov 2026 16:25:54 +0000
Kiryl Shutsemau <kas@kernel.org> wrote:

> On Wed, Nov 04, 2026 at 02:28:06PM -0800, Usama Arif wrote:
> > +#ifdef CONFIG_MITIGATION_ITS
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Returns are patched before alternatives. Align the replacement
> > +	 * source so its return is in the first half of a cache line and ITS
> > +	 * retains its thunk. The copied return can then execute in either
> > +	 * half of a cache line.
> > +	 */
> > +	.pushsection .altinstr_replacement, "ax"
> > +	.balign 64
> > +	.popsection
> > +#endif
> > +	ALTERNATIVE __stringify(cmpq $0x20, %rcx; jb .Lhandle_tail), \
> > +		    __stringify(rep movsb; RET), X86_FEATURE_FSRM  
> 
> Does this mean any RET in a replacement needs the same alignment? Would
> anything catch it if the next one doesn't have it?

My suspicion is that ideally 'yes'.
But someone said (in this thread) that it is worse for RET in the first
half a cache line - so aligning the replacement that way is better.)

> Linus' layout keeps the RET out of the replacement, with no padding:
> 
> 	movq %rdi, %rax
> 	movq %rdx, %rcx
> 	ALTERNATIVE "jmp memcpy_orig", "rep movsb", X86_FEATURE_FSRM
> 	RET
> 
> Non-FSRM takes the same jump it takes today. Does that avoid the ITS
> problem? Why not this layout?

It adds a jump to the non-FSRM path.
Cold cache that is likely to be measurable.
(But not if RET ends up being anything other than 0xc3 -  that will
just kill everything.)

> 
> > +	/*
> > +	 * RCX holds the count for REP MOVSB and scalar copying; RDX is scratch.
> > +	 * Shift RDX to check length >= 2048 without destroying the REP count.
> > +	 * The 10-byte alternative fits a NOP without ERMS.
> > +	 */
> > +	ALTERNATIVE "", __stringify(shrq $11, %rdx; jnz .Lerms), X86_FEATURE_ERMS  
> 
> The RCX/RDX swap is most of the diff, and all it saves is one MOV on
> copies of 2K and up.

Register-register moves don't cost much (they can even be done my the
register renaming logic rather than the alu - but still have to be
fetched and decoded), but needing them is a bit of 'lack of attention
detail' when a simple backtrack can get the value where it is needed.
gcc is very bad at it, a compiler really ought to do a lot better.

David

> 
> What about something like this? Untested.
> 
> diff --git a/arch/x86/lib/memcpy_64.S b/arch/x86/lib/memcpy_64.S
> --- a/arch/x86/lib/memcpy_64.S
> +++ b/arch/x86/lib/memcpy_64.S

>   */

>  	movq %rdi, %rax

> +	ALTERNATIVE "jmp memcpy_orig", "rep movsb", X86_FEATURE_FSRM

>  SYM_FUNC_END(__memcpy)

> @@ -45,11 +43,11 @@ SYM_PIC_ALIAS(memcpy)

> -
>  	cmpq $0x20, %rdx

>

> +
>  	/*
>  	 * We check whether memory false dependence could occur,

Patches Submitted (1)
Last comment 2026-11-10 [PATCH] btrfs: keep a logged extent allocated when its ordered extent fails Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch prevents a logged extent in Btrfs from being freed when its ordered extent fails, which can lead to data corruption and checksum errors during log replay after a transaction abort. The fix involves not freeing the extent of a failed ordered extent that was logged by a fast fsync, but instead keeping it out of the free space cache until the next mount. This ensures that the log replay points to the correct blocks with the correct checksums. The patch also includes additional checks and error handling to prevent similar issues from occurring in other scenarios.

0 participants

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series fixes two bugs in the Linux kernel's swap code. The first bug causes reclaim to skip swapcache folios without anonymous mappings, while the second bug allows cluster tables to be published before allocator locks are reacquired. The patches modify the pageout() function and cluster table publication logic to prevent these issues.

  • [2/3] mm/swap: publish cluster tables after reacquiring allocator locks
  • [3/3] mm/swap: skip relocation of tableless clusters
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses issues in the Linux kernel's swap cache and cluster table management, specifically preventing races between writeout of swapcache folios without anonymous mappings and publication of cluster tables while allocator locks are held. The patches also prevent relocation of tableless clusters, ensuring consistency in memory allocation. The changes were made to address bugs found in previous versions of the swap code.

Discussions / RFCs (0)
No activity

Last comment 2026-11-12 Re: [PATCH] mm: zswap: honor zero cgroup limits after successful reclaim Neutral LLM

Patch proposes to relax zswap.max limit check after successful reclaim, allowing for self-correcting behavior.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Yosry Ahmed ↳ replying to Nhat Pham Neutral LLM
Suggested checking limits again instead of special casing zswap.max = 0 and proposed an alternative approach using obj_cgroup_may_zswap() or a 'try charge' API.
Show original comment 
On Thu, Nov 12, 2026 at 7:45 AM Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com> wrote:
>
> On Wed, Nov 11, 2026 at 5:55 PM Tomás Senart <ts@perfloop.ai> wrote:
> >
> > On Thu, Nov 12, 2026 at 12:28 AM Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org> wrote:
> > > Why is that a problem? Setting memory.zswap.max to 0 while the cgroup
> > > has active swapping processes is naturally racy. The limit can be set
> > > to 0 right after we check it again anyway?
> >
> > Agreed on the race, this doesn't try to fix it.
> >
> > The case here is different: with the limit already at 0, every store
> > writes back an old entry and then stores the new page anyway, until
> > the pool drains. It's bounded, so if it's not worth a special case,
> > happy to drop it.
>
> Yeah I don't think zswap.max = 0 is special here. We can violate the
> limit by changing zswap.max to a value lower than zswap.current. In
> future zswap_store() attempt, we will let it proceed if it shrinks at
> least one page.

Yeah if we will handle this, we should check the limits again instead
of special casing zswap.max = 0, but I am not sure if it's worth
fixing. The simple way would be calling obj_cgroup_may_zswap() again,
but more efficiently we can have obj_cgroup_may_zswap() output the
difference between the limit and current usage, or have a "try charge"
API that handles this internally.

Nhat Pham Positive LLM
Proposed relaxing zswap.max limit check after successful reclaim, arguing it would lead to self-correcting behavior.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-08 Last comment 2026-11-09 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] x86/mce: Restore rate limit on MCE logged message Neutral LLM

This patch restores the rate limit on MCE logged messages, which was inadvertently removed by a previous commit. The rate limit prevents a large number of 'Machine check events logged' messages from being printed to the console in a short period of time, helping to prevent netcons infrastructure overload.

7 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Borislav Petkov Neutral LLM
Raised questions about how the systems in question were being run and whether there was a userspace consumer for hw errors; suggested that the rate limit should be reinstated regardless of the specifics.
Show original comment 
On Sun, Nov 08, 2026 at 02:37:51PM -0500, Rik van Riel wrote:
> FCommit ea324444ece9f moved the "Machine check events logged"
> print from mce_notify_irq() into mce_log() and dropped its
> two-per-minute rate limit as a side effect.
> 
> A particularly unhappy system can spew out around 100 million
> of those "Machine check events logged" messages every hour,
> without that rate limit. A large fleet may have several unhappy
> systems, leaving the netcons infrastructure somewhat flustered.

I need more info here. How are you running those systems?

Usually, correctable errors are consumed by the CEC and you don't see
anything. So you must be doing something else.

.config and dmesg pls.

Thx.

    Boris.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-04 Last comment 2026-11-09 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] xfs: keep successful near rt allocations Neutral LLM

This patch fixes a performance issue in XFS's real-time allocation mechanism by only performing a global search for free space when the initial nearby search fails to find available space.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Carlos Maiolino Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Confirmed that the patch is correct and applied it to the for-next branch.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Nov 04, 2026 at 10:01:15AM -0500, Rik van Riel wrote:
> XFS realtime allocations first attempt to allocate space near where
> other parts of a file already are. If that allocation succeeds, it
> does not have to do a global search for free space.
> 
> Unfortunately, a code cleanup a while ago causes xfs_rtallocate_rtg
> to unconditionally do the global search, even when the search for a
> nearby extent has succeeded.
> 
> Fix it by running the global search only when the near search did
> not find available space.
> 
> Found by noticing higher kernel CPU use and examining the profile.
> 
> Fixes: fd048a1bb391d ("xfs: rework the rtalloc fallback handling")
> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org # v6.12
> Reported-by: Christian Andres Saide <csaide@meta.com>
> Signed-off-by: Rik van Riel <riel@surriel.com>

This sounds correct yes.

Reviewed-by: Carlos Maiolino <cmaiolino@redhat.com>


> ---
>  fs/xfs/xfs_rtalloc.c | 6 +++++-
>  1 file changed, 5 insertions(+), 1 deletion(-)
> 
> diff --git a/fs/xfs/xfs_rtalloc.c b/fs/xfs/xfs_rtalloc.c
> index 84efe5a8fb11..31d90828b4e9 100644
> --- a/fs/xfs/xfs_rtalloc.c
> +++ b/fs/xfs/xfs_rtalloc.c
> @@ -1997,10 +1997,14 @@ xfs_rtallocate_rtg(
>  		if (error == -ENOSPC) {
>  			xfs_rtbuf_cache_relse(&args);
>  			error = 0;
> +			start = 0;
>  		}
>  	}
>  
> -	if (!error) {
> +	/*
> +	 * Global search, runs when the near search did not find space.
> +	 */
> +	if (!error && !start) {
>  		error = xfs_rtallocate_extent_size(&args, minlen, maxlen, &len,
>  				prod, &rtx);
>  	}
> -- 
> 2.56.0
> 
>

13 items
Patches Submitted (7)
[PATCH bpf-next] bpf, cgroup: Don't lose errors in effective BPF_LSM_CGROUP queries Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a bug in the effective BPF_LSM_CGROUP query where errors were being lost due to overwriting of the return value. The fix involves returning -EFAULT immediately when a copy faults, rather than waiting for the loop to complete and potentially losing the error. This ensures that queries with insufficient buffer space or faulty copies are correctly reported as failures.

[PATCH bpf-next 0/3] bpf, cgroup: Don't wait for cgroup_mutex to read or free cgroup bpf state 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] bpf, cgroup: Don't read freed effective arrays in BPF_PROG_QUERY
  • [2/3] bpf, cgroup: Don't attach to a removed cgroup
  • [3/3] bpf, cgroup: Protect cgroup_bpf state with its own mutex
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a performance issue in the Linux kernel where BPF_PROG_QUERY callers, link-info readers, and cgroup bpf release work often wait minutes for the cgroup_mutex lock. The patches introduce a new mutex to protect the cgroup bpf state, reducing contention on the cgroup_mutex. Additionally, two old races are fixed: one in attach and query where the cgroup's bpf state was released between taking the lock, and another in attach where the program was leaked or written to a freed cgroup. The patches have been tested with various configurations and stress tests, showing improved performance and no lockdep or KASAN reports.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-12 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v4] kernfs: allocate inodes and dentry outside kernfs_rwsem Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses long stalls in kernfs due to holding the kernfs_rwsem lock while allocating inodes and dentries. It achieves this by allocating inodes before taking the lock, using kernfs_get_inode_locked(), and only holding the lock around inode lookups. This approach reduces contention on the lock and prevents stalls caused by memory reclaim. The patch also fixes a related issue with kernfs_get_parent_dentry() by holding the lock only during inode lookup. Testing shows significant reductions in wait times for various tasks.

Not a big deal, but one thing changed from v3 that the changelog doesn't
mention. Without an ilookup() first, every caller now needs the
preallocated inode even when one is already hashed. Before this patch
iget_locked() returned a hashed inode without allocating. So when

succeeds without this patch and fails with ENOMEM with it.

failing most other allocations too, so I don't think it's worth
complicating kernfs_get_inode_locked() for. But it is a second case
besides an inactive node where an ilookup() first changes the result.

Last comment 2026-11-12 [PATCH] memcg: flush nr_charged_bytes when the obj stock trylock fails Neutral LLM

The patch addresses a memory accounting issue in the Linux kernel's memcg (memory cgroup) subsystem, where the nr_charged_bytes counter can grow indefinitely when an interrupt handler tries to acquire the obj stock lock and fails. This causes incorrect memory usage reports for tasks within the affected cgroups. The fix involves uncharging the pages stored in nr_charged_bytes whenever the trylock fails.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Muchun Song Acked-by Neutral LLM
Acked the patch without providing any specific feedback or concerns.
Show original comment 

> On Nov 12, 2026, at 09:32, Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev> wrote:
> 
> When interrupt code, such as an RCU callback in softirq, runs on top of
> a task that holds the obj stock lock, its trylock fails and the bytes go
> to objcg->nr_charged_bytes. Nothing empties that counter while every CPU
> using the objcg keeps it in its stock, so it can grow without limit.
> 
> Users see memory.current and the "kernel" field of memory.stat keep
> growing while the cgroup's real usage does not. With memory.high or
> memory.max set, the cgroup is reclaimed, throttled or even OOM-killed
> for memory it does not use. The counter is 32-bit, so after 4 GiB it
> wraps, and those 4 GiB stay charged to the cgroup and its ancestors
> until reboot, even after the cgroup is removed.
> 
> Fix it by uncharging the whole pages in nr_charged_bytes whenever the
> trylock fails.
> 
> Fixes: 200577f69f29 ("memcg: objcg stock trylock without irq disabling")

[PATCH] mm: list_lru: don't use a new memcg's lists for an offline memcg Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a bug in the Linux kernel's memory cgroup (memcg) implementation, specifically in the list_lru code. The issue arises when an offline memcg is removed and its ID is reused by a new memcg. This can lead to incorrect unlinks of folios from the parent memcg's list, causing crashes or silent memory corruption. To fix this, the patch adds a field to store the owning memcg in each list_lru_memcg structure and treats the index as empty when its lists belong to another memcg. The lookup then moves up to the parent, whose list holds the folio.

Last comment 2026-11-12 [PATCH v2] mm: list_lru: fix NULL deref when a folio moves to the root memcg Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a NULL pointer dereference bug that occurs when a folio moves to the root memory cgroup between two lookups. The bug is caused by the slow path of the `folio_memcg_list_lru_alloc` function not checking if the root memcg has its own lists before allocating them. The fix involves adding an additional check for the root memcg using the `memcg_list_lru_allocated` function, similar to the fast path. This prevents a NULL pointer dereference when the folio is moved up to the root between the two lookups.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-12 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm: mglru: splice all generations when reparenting a memcg Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a list_del corruption issue in the Linux kernel's memory management system (mm) by splicing all generations of MGLRU lists when reparenting a memcg (memory control group). The problem occurs when a memcg goes offline and its MGLRU lists are moved to the parent, but only certain generations are moved. This can lead to a folio being left on a freed memcg, causing a use-after-free error. To fix this, the patch moves all MAX_NR_GENS lists, not just those from min_seq to max_seq, ensuring that all folios are properly reparented and avoiding the corruption issue.

Re: [PATCH bpf-next 3/3] bpf, cgroup: Protect cgroup_bpf state with its own mutex Neutral LLM

This patch series addresses a performance issue in the Linux kernel where BPF_PROG_QUERY callers, link-info readers, and cgroup bpf release work often wait minutes for the cgroup_mutex lock. The patches introduce a new mutex to protect the cgroup bpf state, reducing contention on the cgroup_mutex. Additionally, two old races are fixed: one in attach and query where the cgroup's bpf state was released between taking the lock, and another in attach where the program was leaked or written to a freed cgroup. The patches have been tested with various configurations and stress tests, showing improved performance and no lockdep or KASAN reports.

Last comment 2026-11-12 Re: [PATCH] mm: list_lru: don't use a new memcg's lists for an offline memcg Neutral LLM

Patch addresses a bug in list_lru where an offline memcg's lists are used for a new memcg, leading to potential corruption.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Andrew Morton Acked-by Positive LLM
Provided a fix for the bug by requiring the entry to belong to the requested memcg before returning true, and holding RCU over the XArray load and owner comparison.
Show original comment 
On Thu, 12 Nov 2026 18:37:10 -0800 Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev> wrote:

> > [Severity: High]
> > Does memcg_list_lru_allocated() also need to check mlru->memcg == memcg?
> 
> Yes this is correct and Andrew already have a fix for this.
> 

From: Andrew Morton <akpm@linux-foundation.org>
Subject: mm/list_lru: verify memcg owner before skipping allocation
Date: Thu, 12 Nov 2026 16:08:59 -0800

list_lru_from_memcg() rejects an entry at a reused kmemcg_id when it
belongs to another memcg.  memcg_list_lru_allocated() still treats any
entry at that index as a list for the requested memcg.  Its callers can
therefore skip the allocation of that memcg's ancestor lists.

With child C and parent P's lists both unallocated, a reader can retain C
while it is offlined.  If an unrelated new memcg reuses C's ID and
allocates its own list, the reader mistakes that entry for C's and skips
allocation of P's list.  Once the folio's objcg points to P,
deferred_split_folio() can add it to the root list.  If P later gets a
list, unqueue locks P's list while unlinking the folio from root's list,
which can corrupt that list under concurrent operations.

Require the entry to belong to the requested memcg before returning true. 
Hold RCU over the XArray load and owner comparison because this helper is
also called outside the folio fast path's RCU section.

Link: https://lore.kernel.org/20261112161502.18d9d72c0139b8f69bb88ff4@linux-foundation.org
Signed-off-by: Andrew Morton <akpm@linux-foundation.org>
Acked-by: Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev>
Reported-by: <sashiko-bot@kernel.org>
Closes: https://lore.kernel.org/sashiko-outbox-211977@kernel.org
Cc: Chris Mason <mason@kernel.org>
Cc: Dave Chinner <david@fromorbit.com>

+	mlru = xa_load(&lru->xa, idx);
+	allocated = mlru && mlru->memcg == memcg;
+	rcu_read_unlock();

 }

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-12 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] crypto: use vmalloc() for large stream workspaces 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] crypto: zstd - use vmalloc() for stream workspaces Neutral Submitted 2026-11-11Last comment 2026-11-12 Rik van Riel Johannes Weiner
  • [2/2] crypto: deflate - use vmalloc() for stream workspaces Neutral Submitted 2026-11-11Last comment 2026-11-12 Rik van Riel Johannes Weiner
Neutral LLM

The patch series aims to replace the use of kvmalloc() for large stream workspaces in the zstd and deflate compression algorithms with vmalloc(). This change is motivated by the potential memory waste caused by kmalloc's overhead on systems with a large number of CPUs. The patches were tested on various VM configurations, showing significant reductions in workspace backing size without noticeable performance impacts.

Rik van Riel Reviewed-by Positive LLM

Signals: Nice catch
Rik van Riel Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Repeated his Reviewed-by tag for the second patch in the series.
Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Acknowledged the patches' effectiveness in reducing workspace backing size and added his Reviewed-by tag.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Nov 11, 2026 at 07:07:39AM -0800, Usama Arif wrote:
> The zstd acomp streams retain their workspaces after the last transform
> has been freed. The first is allocated during transform initialization;
> additional per-CPU streams are allocated on demand. They are released
> when the algorithm is unregistered.
> 
> In a 4 KiB-page x86-64 build, each stream requests 1,566,281 bytes.
> If kvmalloc() succeeds via kmalloc(), it occupies an order-9 block
> (2 MiB). vmalloc() needs 383 backing pages (1,532 KiB), 516 KiB fewer
> per stream. Across 384 populated streams, that is 193.5 MiB less
> workspace backing if all would have used kmalloc(). There is no such
> gain when kvmalloc() already falls back to vmalloc().
> 
> Use vmalloc() and vfree() directly to avoid the high-order allocation.
> This uses vmalloc address space, page tables and bookkeeping; the
> figures above compare workspace backing only.
> 
> Signed-off-by: Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev>

Nice catch. Given the lifetime of these streams, there really isn't
enough turnover to justify the kmalloc optimization to begin with.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-06 Last comment 2026-11-11 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2] mm/hugetlb: allow crashkernel to reuse hugetlb CMA memory Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch allows the crashkernel to reuse existing HugeTLB CMA memory when requesting additional memory, rather than reserving dedicated CMA areas only. This is particularly useful on systems that already reserve large amounts of memory with hugetlb_cma=, as it enables the crash kernel to use this existing memory as part of its working set. The patch modifies the architecture code to record the requested CMA size and perform the reservation in mm_core_init_early(), making the HugeTLB CMA areas available for reuse while still allowing memblock to reserve any remainder. This approach ensures that the full HugeTLB CMA pool remains available during normal operation, and after a crash, the capture kernel can use the selected ranges as working memory.

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses three issues in the Linux kernel related to Transparent Huge Pages (THP) migration, swap-in operations, and device-private System Map (smaps). The first fix prevents a folio reference leak when concurrent MADV_DONTNEED operations are performed on a PMD. The second fix ensures that PTE swap-in batching does not map poisoned subpages of a large swap-cache folio. The third fix accounts for device-private PMDs using mapcounts in smaps, providing accurate private memory statistics.

---

Swapping in requires checking every subpage, so there would be a cost.
I guess trading it off for correctness :(

---

PG_has_hwpoisoned lives in the second page, so memory_failure() only
sets it once it holds a folio reference, otherwise it could race with a
THP split. If get_hwpoison_page() can't get a reference,
memory_failure() gives up with only the subpage marked. The comment

The error hits a folio that is already in the swap

get_hwpoison_page() fails for any folio that is off the LRU. Normally
memory_failure() would split the folio and isolate the bad page (for
an uncorrected error there is nothing to migrate; that is soft
offline). Here it does neither, and the folio stays in the swap cache
with only the subpage marked.

While looking at this I also noticed that the fallback drops a freshly
read (not yet anon) folio from the swap cache and frees it with the
poisoned page in it. We should actually actually also check folio_test_anon()
before checking folio_has_any_hwpoisoned_page().

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces PMD-level swap entries for anonymous Trans Huge Pages (THPs). The goal is to allow a PMD-mapped THP to be kept mapped across a swap round-trip, which requires modifications to the LoongArch architecture. The approach taken involves adding helpers to manage the exclusive bit in PMDs, enabling the survival of PG_anon_exclusive in swap PMDs.

14 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Usama Arif (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about PMD swap entries being split into PTE-level swap entries during reclaim, explaining that the series introduces a PMD-level swap entry to preserve the huge mapping across the swap round-trip and reduce page-table overhead. The author confirmed that this is the intended behavior and that the patch will proceed with the current implementation.
Show original comment 
When reclaim swaps out a PMD-mapped anonymous THP today, the PMD is
split into HPAGE_PMD_NR PTE-level swap entries via TTU_SPLIT_HUGE_PMD
before unmap. This series introduces a PMD-level swap entry so the
huge mapping can survive the swap round-trip and do_huge_pmd_swap_page()
can restore the PMD mapping directly on swap-in, without waiting for
khugepaged to collapse the range later.

The PMD swap entry is a compact page-table encoding for HPAGE_PMD_NR
consecutive swap slots. swap_map accounting remains per-slot and is
unchanged. A PMD swap entry does not promise that the swap cache always
contains one PMD-sized folio. While the cache is empty or contains one
PMD-sized folio, PMD-level handling can proceed. Once the cache has
split/per-slot state, users either inspect the individual slots directly
(mincore) or split the PMD swap entry and retry through the PTE path
(fault, swapoff, MADV_WILLNEED, UFFDIO_MOVE). MADV_FREE does not consult
the cache: it frees a whole PMD swap entry in place and only splits when
the advised range covers part of the PMD. Likewise, if any slot is still
backed by zswap's per-page store, PMD-order swap-in consumers split and
let the PTE path load the range page by page; an all-on-disk range can
still be read back as one PMD-sized folio.

The series is ordered so every consumer can handle PMD swap entries
before the swap-out producer starts installing them. The swap-out patch
is the last functional change.

Performance (measurements from v8):

  Measured with vm-scalability's case-swap-w-seq benchmark [1]. Four
  pinned workers repeatedly write a 6 GiB anonymous working set on a
  4 vCPU / 4 GiB guest, forcing the set out to swap and back in. Swap
  is an 8 GiB NOCOW raw virtio device (cache=none, aio=native), zswap is
  disabled, and THP enabled/defrag are both "always". The numbers below
  are medians of five interleaved runs per kernel after one warm-up run:

  Metric                          Baseline     Patched      Change
  Aggregate throughput            584.3 MiB/s  2408.9 MiB/s +312.2% (4.12x)
  Elapsed time                    85.43 s      20.72 s      -75.7%
  Major faults                    1,814,699    228,466      -87.4%
  Swap I/O rate                   1.02 GiB/s   4.10 GiB/s   +303.9%

  This swap-intensive synthetic workload measures the reduction in
  swap-fault and page-table overhead from preserving PMD mappings.
  For long-running workloads, I expect benefits from fewer TLB misses,
  less khugepaged work and retaining larger folios. I have not yet found
  a benchmark that captures those benefits well. PMD swap entries also
  move us closer to eliminating page-table deposits for anonymous THPs,
  which would save memory.

Intermediate patches and Sashiko:

  Earlier patches prepare consumers while reclaim still requests
  TTU_SPLIT_HUGE_PMD. PMD swap entries cannot reach those consumers until
  the swap-out producer is enabled. Concerns requiring live PMD swap
  entries before that patch describe an unreachable state. See [2].
  The v8 Sashiko report failed to apply and supplied no findings.

Notes on zswap:

  Native PMD-order zswap load/store is left for a follow-up.
  Alexandre Ghiti is currently working on this.
  This series can still preserve PMD swap entries while zswap is enabled:
  zswap stores the THP as order-0 entries, and PMD-order swap-in
  consumers split any range that has zswap entries before reading it.
  If zswap has written the whole range back to disk, or the swap cache
  still contains one PMD-sized folio, PMD-level handling can proceed.

[1] https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/wfg/vm-scalability.git/tree/case-swap-w-seq
[2] https://lore.kernel.org/all/282ef982-3e48-4283-9155-73a33fc1c4e8@linux.dev/

v2 -> v3: https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260602142537.198755-1-usama.arif@linux.dev/
- Clarified the PMD swap entry rule: it is a compact encoding for
  HPAGE_PMD_NR swap slots, not a guarantee that swap cache always has
  one PMD-sized folio. (Lance Yang)
- Swapoff, fault, MADV_WILLNEED, and UFFDIO_MOVE now classify the
  whole PMD swap-cache range and split/retry through the PTE path for
  split/per-slot cache state. (Lance Yang)
- mincore handles PMD swap entries without assuming one lookup covers
  a split swap-cache range. (Lance Yang)
- UFFDIO_MOVE rechecks all HPAGE_PMD_NR slots before moving an empty
  PMD swap-cache range, avoiding stale rmap metadata for per-slot
  cached folios.
- Added a standalone zswap prerequisite patch from Alexandre that
  distinguishes all-on-disk large-folio ranges from ranges with
  per-page zswap entries.
- Replaced the global zswap-ever-enabled policy with per-range zswap
  checks: PMD swap entries can still be installed while zswap is
  enabled, and PMD-order swap-in consumers split when the range has
  per-page zswap state.
- Added a mincore selftest and updated MADV_WILLNEED coverage so the
  test checks that the PMD swap entry remains in place until first
  touch.  Total pmd_swap coverage is now 14 tests.

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: author provided a clear explanation, confirmed consistency with existing code
Usama Arif (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the s390 architecture's swap PMD layout, explaining that it is an RSTE with its own layout and requiring its own exclusive bit rather than borrowing from the PTE-format macro.
Show original comment 
Prepare for keeping a PMD-mapped anonymous THP mapped by a PMD across the
swap round-trip, which makes PG_anon_exclusive have to survive in a swap
PMD and not just in a swap PTE.

s390 is the one architecture where a swap PMD is not a swap PTE in
disguise: it is an RSTE with its own layout, converted to a fake PTE swap
entry for the common code. Give it its own exclusive bit rather than
borrowing the PTE-format macro, and document it in the RSTE swap layout,
where bit 52 was listed as unused.

Signals: acknowledged feedback, provided explanation
Usama Arif (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the swap PMD layout on x86-64, explaining that it reuses _PAGE_SWP_EXCLUSIVE and asserting that this bit does not alias _PAGE_PSE to prevent an exclusive swap PMD from being present. The author confirmed that this is correct behavior.
Show original comment 
Prepare for keeping a PMD-mapped anonymous THP mapped by a PMD across the
swap round-trip, which makes PG_anon_exclusive have to survive in a swap
PMD and not just in a swap PTE.

x86-64 encodes a swap PMD exactly like a swap PTE, so the new helpers reuse
_PAGE_SWP_EXCLUSIVE, bit 3, which the swap-entry layout already reserves
for PG_anon_exclusive. 32-bit x86 aliases that bit to _PAGE_PSE and does
not select ARCH_HAS_PMD_SOFTLEAVES; assert that, because an exclusive swap
PMD would otherwise be pmd_present().

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: confirmed correct behavior
Usama Arif (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the PMD softleaf layer handling swap entries, agreeing to add support for swap entries as valid PMD softleaves and modifying pmd_softleaf_to_folio() to handle swap entries correctly.
Show original comment 
Reclaim splits a PMD-mapped anonymous THP into PTE-level swap entries
before unmapping it, so an ordinary swap entry has never had to appear in a
PMD. Prepare for installing one there instead: the softleaf layer is where
every consumer decodes non-present PMDs.

Accept swap entries as valid PMD softleaves and add pmd_is_swap_entry().
A swap entry carries no PFN, so make pmd_softleaf_to_folio() warn and
return NULL rather than interpret a swap offset as a page frame number.

Unlike migration and device-private entries, a PMD swap entry can also
carry the swap-exclusive marker, which softleaf_from_pmd() has to strip
before decoding. Strip all three overlays unconditionally while we are
here: each clear is a plain bit clear, so testing first only buys a branch.

Signed-off-by: Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev>
Acked-by: David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Zi Yan <ziy@nvidia.com>

+static void __init pmd_swap_exclusive_tests(struct pgtable_debug_args *args)
+{
+	swp_entry_t entry;
+	softleaf_t softleaf;

+
+	pr_debug("Validating PMD swap exclusive\n");
+	entry = args->swp_entry;
+
+	pmd = softleaf_to_pmd(entry);
+	softleaf = softleaf_from_pmd(pmd);
+
+	WARN_ON(pmd_swp_exclusive(pmd));
+	WARN_ON(!softleaf_is_swap(softleaf));
+	WARN_ON(memcmp(&entry, &softleaf, sizeof(entry)));
+
+	pmd = pmd_swp_mkexclusive(pmd);
+	softleaf = softleaf_from_pmd(pmd);
+
+	WARN_ON(!pmd_swp_exclusive(pmd));
+	WARN_ON(!softleaf_is_swap(softleaf));
+	WARN_ON(pmd_swp_soft_dirty(pmd));
+	WARN_ON(memcmp(&entry, &softleaf, sizeof(entry)));
+
+	pmd = pmd_swp_clear_exclusive(pmd);
+	softleaf = softleaf_from_pmd(pmd);
+
+	WARN_ON(pmd_swp_exclusive(pmd));
+	WARN_ON(!softleaf_is_swap(softleaf));
+	WARN_ON(memcmp(&entry, &softleaf, sizeof(entry)));
+}
+#else  /* !CONFIG_ARCH_HAS_PMD_SOFTLEAVES */

Last comment 2026-11-13 [PATCH] Revert "sched/fair: Add SIS_UTIL support to select_idle_capacity()" Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch reverts a previous change that added SIS_UTIL support to the select_idle_capacity() function in the Linux kernel's scheduler. The reverted change caused issues when the scan budget was zero, leading to incorrect idle CPU selection and increased latency for certain workloads. The patch restores the original behavior of searching the allowed domain for an idle CPU, which can increase scan CPU cost but ensures accurate idle CPU selection. This change is intended to fix a performance regression introduced by the previous commit.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer Andrea Righi requested additional testing to compare the performance of symmetric idle-selection path with SIS_UTIL enabled and disabled, to determine if disabling SIS_UTIL produces similar improvements in other scenarios. Reviewer Andrea Righi expressed concern that full scans in select_idle_capacity() could be costly on large systems with many CPUs in the same Last Level Cache (LLC), and requested further consideration.

Rik Riel ↳ replying to Usama Arif Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Rik Riel questioned whether the asymmetrical selection should prioritize any CPU 'close enough' to the required capacity (X) when dealing with SMT and power-driven hardware scaling, suggesting a threshold of 3/4 of X as a compromise between preventing overloading small cores and allowing tasks to run immediately.
Show original comment 
Afaik, the asymmetrical selection is supposed
to solve a very specific problem: when a task
requires X amount of CPU capacity to run, we
try to avoid scheduling it on a CPU that has
only a fraction of X available.

This creates the issue that a task that appears
to "need" exactly X, because it's been running
on a CPU with full available capacity, ends up
not being scheduled at all when the available
idle core has X-3% available capacity, because
that CPU happens to spend 3% of its time handling
interrupts, or running something else.

In that situation, it will be better to run the
task on the CPU with 97% capacity right now,
than to not run the task until a CPU frees up
later.

Things like SMT and power-driven hardware
scaling complicate the matter further, because
we simply cannot see the full amount of capacity 
that is available on each CPU.

That makes me wonder if we should simply accept
any CPU that's "close enough to X" when dealing
with asymmetrical placement.

Say, anything that's above 3/4 of the capacity
of X would be considered good enough to run the task.

That still prevents us from placing a CPU intensive
task on a little core in a big.LITTLE system, or
from placing a task on a CPU that spends 80% of its
time handling interrupts, but it will allow a task
to run right now, when the capacity imbalance is small.

Does that make sense, or am I forgetting something
important?

-- 
All Rights Reversed.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-04 Last comment 2026-11-10 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2] x86/lib: Use REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes large memory copies on ERMS CPUs without FSRM by using the REP MOVSB instruction from 2048 bytes onwards, reducing execution time by up to 66.8%. The patch also folds memcpy_orig() into __memcpy() and makes adjustments for FSRM CPUs. Smaller copies continue to use a scalar loop for performance reasons.

10 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer David Laight noted that the patch does not handle misaligned copies of size 512 to 640 bytes correctly, which were slower than the scalar loop in v1, and requested raising the threshold from 512 to 2048 bytes. Reviewer David Laight questioned the patch's assumption that REP MOVSB would always improve performance on ERMS CPUs, citing specific alignment issues on Zen and Intel CPUs, including a case where REP MOVSB is slower due to misalignment between source and destination buffers.
Show original comment

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-12 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v3 0/2] x86/lib: Use REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] x86/lib: Keep the scalar memcpy() count in RCX Submitted 2026-11-11
  • [2/2] x86/lib: Use REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM Neutral Submitted 2026-11-11Last comment 2026-11-12 Peter Zijlstra Borislav Petkov David Laight
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series optimizes the x86 memcpy() function for ERMS CPUs without FSRM by using REP MOVSB for large copies (2048 bytes and larger). The approach taken is to split the changes into two patches: one that prepares the scalar register allocation, and another that adds the ERMS size check and adopts Linus's FSRM entry layout. This allows REP MOVSB to replace the existing jump to memcpy_orig(), keeping RET in ordinary text. The patch also addresses issues related to return handling and register reloads.

David Laight ↳ replying to Borislav Petkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer David Laight noted that the patch does not handle the case where the count in RCX is zero, which would cause REP MOVSB to execute indefinitely. The reviewer suggested that instead of creating multiple functions, the constant in the instruction could be patched directly, and proposed a non-standard calling convention for the function to allow patching the call site with 'rep movsb; nop3'. They also mentioned that a special function to handle patching would make sense.
Show original comment 
On Wed, 11 Nov 2026 15:46:08 -0800
Borislav Petkov <bp@alien8.de> wrote:

---

Multiple functions seems like a good idea.

In some cases you can patch the constant in the instruction.
That is safe here even if a multi-byte constant crosses a cache line
because it is only an optimisation.

Patching the call sites would certainly be cleaner.
More interesting would be having a function with a non-standard calling
convention (%rdi, %rsi, %rcx, everything else preserved, void result)
so that the 5 byte call can be patched with "rep movsb; nop3".
I can't see anywhere that uses the result.
Maybe start with "rep movsb; nop3" and patch with the call?
You'd still need a normal memcpy() for the calls gcc makes and if the
address is taken.

A special function to do the patching would also make sense.
All Intel non-atom cpu since sandy bridge run 'rep movsb' as fast
as 'rep movsq' - so you only need to worry about short copies.
Whereas some of the older Zen cpu run misaligned copies slowly.
So you could pick/patch the most appropriate code using more complex
logic than just whether one of the X86_FEATURE_xxx bits is set.

The same calling convention might make sense for memmove().
Although it probably wants a scratch register for the (dst - src < len)
check (it might want dst in the scratch register!).

David

Re: [PATCH v2] mm/hugetlb: allow crashkernel to reuse hugetlb CMA memory Neutral LLM
Last comment 2026-11-13 Re: [PATCH] Revert "sched/fair: Add SIS_UTIL support to select_idle_capacity()" Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch reverts a previous change that added SIS_UTIL support to the select_idle_capacity() function in the Linux kernel's scheduler. The reverted change caused issues when the scan budget was zero, leading to incorrect idle CPU selection and increased latency for certain workloads. The patch restores the original behavior of searching the allowed domain for an idle CPU, which can increase scan CPU cost but ensures accurate idle CPU selection. This change is intended to fix a performance regression introduced by the previous commit.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Andrea Righi ↳ replying to Usama Arif Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Andrea Righi noted that reverting the SIS_UTIL support requires a separate patch and a system reboot, as there is no existing runtime switch to control idle selection mode. The reviewer notes that the regression points to a limitation of SIS_UTIL, suggesting that incorporating observed idle CPU availability into the budget calculation could help distinguish actual saturation from high utilization with available CPUs. Reviewer Andrea Righi expressed concern about the potential high scan cost in select_idle_capacity() under frequent wakeups when few or no CPUs are idle, but did not provide a measured CPU-cost figure.
Show original comment 
Yeah, this would require a patch and a reboot. Unfortunately there isn't
an existing runtime switch to force symmetric/asym idle selection.

---

That's a substantial difference, I agree the regression should be
addressed. Perhaps this points to a limitation of the SIS_UTIL
heuristic?

We could investigate incorporating observed idle CPU availability into
the SIS_UTIL budget calculation, rather than relying solely on aggregate
utilization. Periodic load balancing already collects idle CPU
statistics, which might help distinguish actual saturation from high
utilization with CPUs still available.

Prateek, do you have any thoughts on this? Do you see a way to improve
SIS_UTIL for this workload while keeping the symmetric and asymmetric
paths consistent?

I don't have a measured CPU-cost figure for this case. My concern is
the worst-case scan cost under frequent wakeups when few or no CPUs
are idle.

Thanks,
-Andrea

Last comment 2026-11-13 Re: [PATCH v3 2/2] x86/lib: Use REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the x86 memcpy function to use REP MOVSB for large memory copies on ERMS CPUs without FSRM support. The patch introduces a new helper function, memcpy_erms, which uses REP MOVSB when the copy size is greater than or equal to 2048 bytes. This approach aims to improve performance by taking advantage of the optimized instruction set available on ERMS CPUs. The patch also includes alternative implementations for different CPU features and provides a fallback to the original memcpy implementation when necessary.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David Laight ↳ replying to Usama Arif Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer David Laight requested that the patch be revised to use a more maintainable approach, specifically suggesting the use of tail jumps instead of adding a helper CALL/RET to every path. Reviewer David Laight suggested an alternative implementation of memcpy() that uses a single assembly instruction for all CPUs, and provided example code to illustrate the approach. He also made some minor suggestions for optimization.
Show original comment 
On Fri, 13 Nov 2026 06:11:36 -0800
Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev> wrote:

---

I was thinking more of something like:
(written in the email so untested)

static always_inline void *memcpy(void *dst, const void *src, size_t len)
{
	void *orig_dst = dst;

	asm volatile(	".pushsection ".memcpy_calls\n"
			"\t.long . - 1f\n"
			".popsection\n"
			"1:\trep movsb\n"
			"\tnop; nop"
			: "+c (len), "+D" (dst), "+S" (src)
			:: ASM_CALL_CONSTRAINT);

	return orig_dst;
}

An unused return value will be optimised away at all the call sites.

That will work (maybe slowly) on all cpu.
Then some code reads all the addresses from .memcpy_calls and, if necessary,
patches the 5 bytes to a call to the appropriate function.

It might be worth giving the code one or two scratch registers so that
a short copy loop doesn't need to spill to stack (perhaps %rax and %rdx).
It'll matter less for a longer copy loop.

So you might have:
_memcpy_erms:
	cmp $nnnn, %rcx
	jb  _memcpy_words
	rep movsb
	RET

_memcpy_words:
	cmpb $nnnn %rcx
	jb  _memcpy_bytes
	mov %rax,%rcx
	or  %rcx,%rdi
	or  %rcx,%rsi
	test $7,%rcx
	jnz _memcpy_misaligned
	sub $8,%rax
1:	mov %rcx,[%rsi]
	mov %rdx,8[%rsi]
	mov [%rdi],%rcx
	mov 8[%rdi],%rdx
	add $16,%rdi
	add $16,%rsi
	sub $16,%rax
	jnc 1b:
	jz  2f
	mov %rax,[%si]
	mov [%rdi]%rax]
2:	RET

That isn't the best 2-word loop by any means.
And you may not care about misaligned pointers.
	
etc

David

Signals: requested changes, alternative implementation, minor suggestions
Linus Torvalds ↳ replying to David Laight Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Linus Torvalds noted that many memcpy() calls are not actual function calls on a C level and should be handled by the compiler, which can turn small copies into loads and stores. He also stated that hiding 'memcpy()' from the compiler for performance reasons is inefficient and cannot catch all cases.
Show original comment 
The problem with this approach two-fold:

 - many memcpy() calls aren't actually memcpy() calls on a C level

 - many memcpy() that *are* calls on a C level should never be calls
in the generated code

That first case is for things like structure copies etc. And it's
common, and it's important, although for the kernel it's usually a
non-issue because it's *typicay* a small constant-sized copy, which
brings us to the second case...

The second case is hugely important. The compiler needs to turn small
memcpy() calls into just loads and stores. Calling "memcpy()" for a
known 64-byte copy is hugely inefficient.

We used to - long ago - do that "constant small sizes" thing by hand.
It was a huge pain. We now rely on the compiler to do it.

End result: we must *not* hide 'memcpy()' from the compiler for
performance reasons, and we cannot catch all cases anyway because the
compiler generates implicit memcpy calls.

This is why I really wish we had the compiler just generate a
different sequence for 'call memcpy'. One that used a different
callign convention - preferably just the 'rep movs' one so that we
could just rewrite the call with 'rep movsb' in place.

Without compiler support, we're kind of screwed.

              Linus

              Linus
View on lore ↗
Signals: compiler support, performance reasons
David Laight ↳ replying to Linus Torvalds Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer David Laight pointed out that the alternative code path for ERMS CPUs without FSRM should use a cmp immediate of 32 bytes instead of 2048, as changing it from 2048 to 32 made aligned warmed copies 16.5% slower. Reviewer suggested that instead of using REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM, the patch could detect small constant sizes and call the real memcpy() function, which would allow the compiler to inline them as a series of moves. Reviewer David Laight expressed concern that the use of REP MOVSB in memcpy_erms function may not be optimal due to its non-standard calling convention, which could lead to issues on certain architectures.
Show original comment 
On Fri, 13 Nov 2026 09:34:26 -0800
Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org> wrote:

---

Actually you could detect the 'small constant sizes' and call the real
memcpy for them.
The compiler would then (hopefully) inline them as a series of moves.

---

I knew you had wished that in the past.
It isn't as though it doesn't have other calls with (annoying)
non-standard calling conventions.
For one arch we optimise 'shift by count' with a jump table, and fall
foul of needing a scratch register - so have to spill to stack.

David
